Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Note
The Annual Report 2021 was examined and adopted by the
Supervisory Board of the National Bank of Moldova at meeting of
May 27, 2022 and was submitted to the Parliament of the
Republic of Moldova in accordance with the provisions of Law No.
548/1995 on the National Bank of Moldova (republished in
Monitorul Oﬁcial of the Republic of Moldova, 2015, No. 297-300,
art. 544, with subsequent amendments).
The report was compiled using the latest statistical data held by
the National Bank of Moldova, the National Bureau of Statistics,
the Ministry of Economy and Infrastructure and the Ministry of
Finance.
Also, were used data provided by international organizations and
central banks of neighboring countries.
Computation of some statisitcal data was conducted by the
National Bank of Moldova.
All rights reserved. No part of this publication may be reproduced,
and the use of data in studies is allowed with the proper
speciﬁcation of the source.

Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Foreword
2021 was a year of recovery for Moldova’s economy, and at the
same time, a year in which the economic situation and not just
the economic one, both internationally and domestically, changed
dramatically.
The ﬁrst half of the year was marked by the pandemic crisis and
restrictions related to the epidemiological situation, which further
affected economic activity, people’s lives, and consumption.
Nevertheless, as the effects of the pandemic crisis eased, efforts
to support the economy in most countries of the world began to
materialize, with the economy recovering rapidly, accompanied
by rising inﬂation, as resources were absorbed.
The second half of the year could be characterized by a complete
change in the international economic context. Several tensions
and risks, accumulated over time, have generated the energy
crisis, the accelerated increase in food prices, as well as the
worsening of the geopolitical situation in the region.
The
consequences of these events have been felt here in the form of
shocks and pressures on prices.
Despite these factors, Moldova’s economy saw record gross
domestic product growth of 13.9% in 2021 compared to the
previous year. This unprecedented growth was due to inﬂation,
which recorded a sharp increase during the year.
The systemic and deep reforms conducted over the past years
in the banking sector have contributed to achieving a high level
of resilience and stability. Thus, banks have not only weathered
the pandemic crisis without signiﬁcant difﬁculties but have also
been part of the support mechanisms for the population and the
economy, ready to support economic growth through lending.
The level of the National Bank’s ofﬁcial reserve assets continued
to increase during the year, reaching USD 3.9 billion.
Their


Foreword
5
volume ensures a sufﬁcient and comfortable level of coverage for
international business operations and protection against possible
internal and external shocks in order to maintain the stability and
resilience of the national currency.
Against the backdrop of recent simultaneous crises, the impact of
which is increasing, the National Bank is making efforts to mitigate
the consequences of shocks on prices, to maintain a balance that
would simultaneously meet restrictive monetary policy conditions
and stimulative conditions for economic growth, as well as to
ensure ﬁnancial and currency stability. Efforts that will lead, once
the crises are overcome and the risks of external shocks are
reduced, to a rapid recovery of the economy and the resumption
of sustainable growth.
Octavian Armas, u
Governor


6
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Summary
The international context
In 2021, the global economy began to recover from the COVID-19
pandemic crisis, even as social and economic constraints during
periods of acute need were reactivated. The recovery in advanced
economies has been more robust than in emerging and developing
economies, against the backdrop of more stimulative monetary
and ﬁscal policies and much higher vaccination coverage. The
recovery of global demand at a faster pace than supply has
pushed up producer and consumer prices to their highest levels
in decades.
This has prompted a tightening of monetary and
ﬁscal conditions to the extent that they slow price growth but
do not undermine the still fragile economic recovery. One factor
driving up costs has been the disruption of the global transport
and logistics system in the wake of the pandemic crisis. Also, the
faster recovery in global oil demand, given that OPEC+ production
capacity has been limited, has led to oil prices increasing by an
average of 65.0%. The ampliﬁcation of the gas crisis in Europe has
caused European natural gas prices to rise to USD 2,000/1,000
cubic meters in December 2021. Although the harvest of some
agricultural products has been relatively satisfactory, increasing
ﬁnancial, energy, transport, storage and logistics costs have led
to signiﬁcant increases in global food prices.
Most emerging
economies’ currencies trended slightly lower in the second half
of the year amid talk of raising Federal Reserve System interest
rates. The Turkish lira depreciated the most due to non-traditional
economic policies.
The developments of economic activity
in the Republic of Moldova
In 2021, GDP increased by 13.9% compared to 2020, mostly
supported by domestic demand from the population in the context
of increasing disposable income, a favourable environment with
relatively low interest rates, a rich harvest, and positive economic
activity in the region, which has gradually recovered from the
adverse effects associated with the pandemic. Structurally, this
dynamic was mainly determined by developments in consumption
and
gross
capital
formation.
In
this
respect,
household
consumption was 15.5% higher than in the previous year,
determined mainly by the increase in household disposable
income
and
credit
growth,
while
gross
capital
formation
contributed 5.7 percentage points to GDP growth.
Exports
increased by 17.5% compared to 2020, as external demand
recovered, and the agricultural sector had a good harvest. Public
administration consumption increased by 2.0% compared to the
previous year.
Increasing domestic demand led to a positive
dynamic in imports, which were 19.2% higher than in the previous


Summary
7
year.
Within the sectors of the national economy, the most
pronounced positive impact was exerted by the dynamics of the
agricultural
sector.
This2,
in
the
context
of
favourable
agrometeorological conditions, registered an increase of 45.0%
compared to 2020.
At the same time, a pronounced positive
contribution was determined by the increase in gross value added
in trade and transport, sectors that recovered after being severely
affected
by
the
2020
COVID-19
restrictions.
Positive
developments
were
also
recorded
in
ﬁnancial,
industrial,
communications, social assistance, and medical sectors. On the
other hand, a negative dynamic in 2021 can be seen in the
construction sector.
The evolution of inﬂation
In 2021, the average annual CPI inﬂation rate was 5.1%, 1.3
percentage points higher than in 2020. During 2021, the annual
inﬂation rate showed a signiﬁcant upward trend. It increased from
0.4% in December 2020 to 13.9% in December 2021. The upward
trend in the annual inﬂation rate was supported by higher prices
for food, oil, and other commodities on the international market,
which was reﬂected in higher prices for these products on the local
market. A pronounced impact was determined by the adjustment
of gas and heat tariffs in November 2021 in the context of rising
natural gas prices on the regional market.
At the same time,
the increase in inﬂation in 2021 was supported by demand-side
pressures against the background of rising disposable income of
the population, as well as increased lending. Inﬂationary pressures
were also exerted by exchange rate dynamics in 2021. The good
harvest in 2021 temporarily delayed, during the summer months,
the propagation of the effects of the sharp rise in food prices
observed on the international and regional markets. Subsequently,
however, in the last months of the year, with the substitution of
domestic fruits and vegetables by imported ones, food prices on
the domestic market accelerated markedly, generating the largest
impact on the annual CPI rate.
By the end of 2021, major deviations had accumulated in several
regulated price categories at both central and local government
levels.
Over the last 5-10 years, these services have been
subsidized directly or indirectly by local budgets, and some
service
providers
have
accumulated
signiﬁcant
negative
deviations due to non-adjustment of tariffs to costs. It should be
noted that these inﬂationary pressures may materialize in the
future and will contribute to the evolution of inﬂation both through
direct effects and secondary effects on other goods and services.
The promotion of monetary policy
The conduct of monetary policy during 2021 can be divided into
two distinctive periods. In the ﬁrst period, from the beginning of
the year to the end of July, monetary policy decisions were geared
towards mitigating the impact of the pandemic on economic
2Agriculture, forestry and ﬁshing.


8
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
activity by creating the right monetary conditions to help bring
inﬂation back within ±1.5 percentage points from the 5.0% target,
aiming to maintain sufﬁcient liquidity, and lowering the cost of
bank credit conducive to supporting domestic demand, including
lending activity, and stimulating consumption and investment.
The
NBM
decreased
the
minimum
reserves
required
from
Moldovan lei deposits from 32.0% to 26.0%.
The reserve
requirement
for
freely
convertible
foreign
currency
was
maintained at 30.0% in 2021.
The decisions were aimed at
increasing the liquidity available to licensed banks to prevent
liquidity risk and strengthen the stability of Moldova’s banking
sector.
The second half of 2021 was inﬂuenced by the ampliﬁcation of
global, regional and national risks and uncertainties generated by
the recovery from the COVID-19 pandemic crisis, the uncertainty
associated with the global energy crisis, and the outlook for
economic conditions in the context of these events was precarious.
Thus, in the second half of the year, the NBM gradually tightened
monetary policy with the aim of mitigating inﬂationary pressures
generated by the second round effects of global price increases,
production and distribution costs of energy resources and food. At
the same time, inﬂationary pressures were also generated by
domestic aggregate demand supported by increases in household
income (payroll,
social assistance,
remittances,
etc.)
and
increases in consumer and mortgage credit.
In this context, the Executive Board of the NBM decided to
gradually increase the monetary policy interest rate from 2.65%
to 6.50% annually. The aim of increasing the base rate was to
create the monetary conditions necessary to moderate the growth
rates of consumer prices.
The annual growth rate of broad money supply in 2021 was 11.3%.
The M2 monetary aggregate increased by 9.5% annually, money
in circulation increased by 6.3% annually. The average interest
rate on new loans granted in national currency in 2021 was 7.40%
annually, while the average rate on new deposits attracted was
3.48% annually.
The banking system
As of 31.12.2021, in the Republic of Moldova there were 11 banks
licensed by the National Bank of Moldova (NBM).
The banking sector, during 2021, according to the data presented
by the banks, is characterized by high liquidity, growth of assets,
loans, own funds, deposits of individuals and legal entities. In
addition, the proﬁt for the year compared to the previous year
increased to the pre-pandemic size.
Although
there
was
a
slight
increase
in
the
amount
of
non-performing loans, the quality of the loan portfolio improved
compared to the end of the previous year, due to the higher
growth rate of the loan portfolio compared to non-performing
loans, as well as the decrease in the share of overdue loans by 1.4
percentage points to 3.1%.


Summary
9
The National Bank conducts ex ofﬁcio supervision under the
annual prudential supervision programme.
For 2021,
the
prudential supervision programme included various ongoing
actions (monitoring of key indicators, analysis of ﬁnancial reports
submitted by banks,
external audit reports,
monitoring of
remediation plans following on-site inspections), as well as actions
for the preparation of the SREP report (assessment of business
model, analysis of governance and internal control, assessment of
recovery plans, etc.).
During 2021,
7 planned thematic on-site inspections were
conducted
at
licensed
banks.
To
remove
violations
and
deﬁciencies found during the controls, supervisory measures were
ordered against two banks and one bank was sanctioned with a
warning.
In 2021, the banking sector continued the trend of consolidation
of own funds, which amounted to MDL 15.2 billion, increasing by
11.2%.
At the same time, all banks comply with the requirement against
the indicator "Total own funds rate", including with capital buffers.
The total own funds rate in the banking sector according to the
data submitted by licensed banks is 25.9%, decreasing by 1.4
percentage points compared to the end of the previous year.
In 2021, the NBM continued its supervision of banks under the
SREP supervision and evaluation process. Thus, the results of the
supervision and evaluation process (SREP) were approved by the
Executive Board of the NBM with the imposition of speciﬁc capital
requirements to each bank according to its individual risk proﬁle,
which are also complied with.
At the same time, banks maintained a high level of liquidity and
proﬁtability.
Thus, the value of the long-term liquidity indicator (Principle I of
liquidity) was 0.74 (limit ≤1), practically at the same level as at
the end of the previous year.
Current liquidity by sector (Principle II of liquidity) was 48.6% (limit
≥20%), down 2.1 percentage points from the end of the previous
year.
Also, as of 31.12.2021, all banks complied with Principle III of
liquidity, which shows the ratio of adjusted effective liquidity to
required liquidity per maturity band (limit ≥1).
The indicator of liquidity coverage by sector was 358.5%, showing
an increase of 40.5 percentage points compared to the end of the
previous year. The given indicator was also met by all banks (limit
≥70% – from 1 January 2021).
The proﬁt related to the ﬁnancial year ended on 31.12.2021 in
the banking sector was MDL 2.3 billion, increasing compared to
the previous year by 53.3%. For the year 2021, the return on
assets3 and on capital4 of licensed banks recorded values of 2.0%
and respectively 12.3%, increasing respectively by 0.5 percentage
3Return on assets=annualized net income/average assets.
4Return on capital=annualized net income/average share capital.


10
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
points and by 3.6 percentage points compared to the end of the
previous year.
Assets and deposits also continued to grow, respectively by 14.2%
to MDL 118.5 billion and by 13.1% to MDL 90.1 billion. Compared
to the end of the previous year, the credit portfolio in the banking
sector increased by 23.5% to MDL 56.4 billion. During the year,
the share of non-performing loans in the loan portfolio decreased
by 1.2 percentage points, constituting 6.1%.

Chapter 1
International context
1.1
Global
economy,
ﬁnancial
and
commodity markets
In 2021, the world economy began to recover from the COVID-19
pandemic crisis, even though social and economic constraints
during worsening periods were reactivated.
The recovery in
advanced economies has been more robust than in emerging and
developing economies, amid continued stimulating monetary and
ﬁscal policies and a much greater degree of vaccination.
The
recovery of world demand at a faster pace than supply has led to
rising production and consumer prices to the highest levels for the
last decades. This has tightened monetary and ﬁscal conditions to
the extent that they have slowed down prices but not affected the
still fragile economic recovery.
A factor in the increase in costs

has been the disruption of the global transport and logistics
system as a consequence of the pandemic crisis. Also, the faster
recovery of world oil demand, given that production capacity
OPEC+ was limited, led to an increase in oil prices by an average
of 65.0%.
The escalation of the gas crisis in Europe caused
European gas prices to rise to USD 2,000/1,000 cubic meters in
December 2021.
Although the harvest of some agricultural
products was relatively satisfactory, the increase in ﬁnancing,
energy, and transport, warehousing and logistics costs led to a
signiﬁcant increase in world food prices. Most emerging market
currencies showed a slight downward trend in the second half of
the year, amid discussions on rising interest rates FRS.
The
Turkish lira depreciated the most because of non-traditional
economic policies.
In 2021, most economies experienced robust economic growth
due to a signiﬁcantly low base in 2020. Overall, in 2021 world
economy grew by 5.9%, after a decline of 3.1% registered in
20205. Advanced economies grew by an average of 5.0%, after
contracting by an average of 4.5% in 2020. Thus, the US economy
grew by 5.7%, Japan by 1.7%, the United Kingdom by 7.5% and
the Euro area by 5.2%. Emerging and developing economies
grew by an average of 6.5% in 2021, after contracting by 2.0%
in 2020. Thus, China’s economy grew by 8.1% and the Russian
Federation by 4.7%. Growth in neighbouring economies and main
trading partners was 5.6% in Romania and 3.1% in Ukraine (Chart
1.1).
In 2021, US dollar, overall, appreciated against other currencies
of international circulation, especially in the second half of 2021.
Thus, the USD index rose to an average of 96 in November 2021.
The key factor in the appreciation of the US dollar was the decrease
5IMF, World Economic Outlook Update January 2022.


12
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
in the volume of purchases of Federal Reserve assets and the
forecasts for the increase in rates in 2022. If in the ﬁrst half of
2021, the Federal Reserve insisted that inﬂationary pressures are
temporary and that the US economy has not yet fully recovered
from the crisis, in the second half, raise of the annual inﬂation rate
to 7.0% in December 2021 and lowering the unemployment rate
to 3.9% convinced Federal Reserve governors that it is high time
to reduce monetary stimulus and subsequently tighten monetary
conditions in 2022 to return to the target level of inﬂation of 2.0%
annual average. Other factors in the appreciation of the US dollar
in 2021 include the high rate of vaccination of the population, the
accelerated decrease of social and economic restrictions and the
continuation of pro-cyclical ﬁscal policies.

BRL – Brazilian real, BYR – Belarus rouble, CNY – Chinese

rand
The currencies of advanced economies had a mixed evolution
in 2021, depending on the economic evolution, the monetary
conditions in relation to those of FRS, the level of inﬂation and the
degree of progression of the vaccination process of the population.
Thus, the single European currency depreciated against the
US dollar by an average of 3.5% during the year and by 7.7%
on an annual basis at the end of the year, because the annual
inﬂation in the euro area rose to 5.0% (December 2021), but the
ECB continued stimulating monetary policy in pandemic volumes.
The pound sterling appreciated against the US dollar by an
average of 6.7% during 2021, recovering the drastic losses related
to the pandemic crisis in 2020 and beneﬁting from the much faster
relaxation of social and economic restrictions related to COVID-19.
Also, in December 2021, the Bank of England increased the base
rate from 0.1 to 0.25%. The Japanese yen depreciated against
the US dollar by an average of 2.8% during the year and by 9.7% in
December on an annual basis. The evolution of several currencies
of advanced economies can be viewed in the table 1.1.
The evolution of the currencies of emerging and developing
economies was as usual, much more dispersed.
Thus, the
Turkish lira saw a signiﬁcant depreciation due to non-traditional
policies on the correlation between interest rates and inﬂation. It
depreciated against the US dollar by an average of 26.8% during
2021, and the value in December 2021 was 75.3% lower than the
previous year. Annual inﬂation in Turkey increased from 15.0% in
January 2021 to 36.1% in December 2021, and the Central Bank
of Turkey gradually decreased its base rate since September 2021
from 19.0% to 14.0%.
In addition, the Argentine peso has
continued to depreciate due to the problems of state debt
sustainability
in
recent
years.
In
the
region,
most
inﬂation-targeting economies increased rates in the second half of
the year to counter signiﬁcant inﬂationary pressures.
The
Romanian leu appreciated against the US dollar by an average
of 2.0% during the year but depreciated by 9.5% on an annual
basis in December 2021. Polish zloty appreciated against US
dollar by 0.9% on average during the year but depreciated 11.0%
on an annual basis in December 2021.
The Russian rouble
depreciated against the US dollar by an average of 2.4% during
the year but appreciated by 0.4% on an annual basis in December
2021. Ukrainian hryvnia depreciated against the US dollar by
an average of 1.2% during the year but appreciated by 3.4% on
an annual basis in December 2021.
For comparison, the
Moldovan leu appreciated against the US dollar by an average
of 2.1% during the year and by 2.8% compared to the end of
2020.
In the table 1.1 the evolution of several currencies of
emerging and developing economies can be viewed.


Chapter 1. International context
13
The recovery of world demand at a faster pace than supply has
led to rising production and consumer prices to the highest levels
in the last decades. The increase in costs in the ﬁrst stage was
determined by the disruption in the global transport and logistics
system following the pandemic crisis.
In addition, the faster

recovery of world oil demand, given that OPEC+ production
capacity was limited, led to an increase in oil prices by an average
of 65.6%. The ampliﬁcation of the gas crisis in Europe has caused
European gas prices to rise to USD 2,000/1,000 cubic meters in
December 2021. Overall, in 2021, the quotations for imported
natural gas in the Republic of Moldova increased by 105.3%
compared to 2020. Base metals rose by an average of 46.8%, and
precious metals by only 3.7% as pandemic risks fell and liquidity
rose. Although the harvest of some agricultural products has been
relatively satisfactory, the increase in ﬁnancing, energy, transport,
warehousing and logistics costs has led to a signiﬁcant increase in
world food prices. According to the FAO index, food prices on the
international market increased in 2021 on average by 28.2%, of
which oils by 65.8%, sugar by 37.5%, cereals by 27.2%, dairy
products by 17.0% and meat by 12.9% (Chart 1.3)
Oil prices in 2021 were inﬂuenced by rising oil demand faster than
supply. From August 2021, OPEC and its allies decided to increase
production by 400 thousand barrels per day per month until 2022,
to counter the rise in gasoline prices, amid fears that rising inﬂation
could undermine the economic recovery after COVID-19. Thus,
average monthly OPEC production in 2021 was 31.6 million
barrels per day, up 2.9% from 30.7% in 2020, but down 8.7% from
pre-pandemic OPEC production. The US and other oil-producing
countries also increased production compared to 2020 but did not
reach pre-pandemic supply levels. At the same time, world oil
consumption increased in 2021 on average by 5.7% compared to
1.7% as world oil production increased (Chart 1.4).

1.2
Evolution of important economies
United States of America
In 2021, the US gross domestic product increased by 5.7%, after
contracting by 3.4% in 2020. The average unemployment rate
in the US was 5.4%, down from 6.3% at the beginning of the
year to 3.9% at the end of the year. The average inﬂation rate
represented 4.6%, reaching a high of 7.0% in December 2021.
Industrial production increased by 5.6%, after a decline of 7.2% in
2020.

Euro area
In 2021, the Euro area economy grew by 5.2%, after declining
by 6.6% in 2020. Industrial production in the Euro area grew by
7.8%, of which in Germany – by 4.0% and in France – by 6.0%.
Construction production increased by an average of 5.2%, imports
by 21.4% and exports by 14.1%. The average unemployment rate
for 2021 was 7.7% compared to 8.0% in 2020. As proinﬂationary
pressures intensiﬁed, consumer prices in the Euro area rose by an
average of 2.6% in 2021, and inﬂation was already 5.0% at the
end of the year (Table 1.2).

and
main
trading
partners
Romania
In 2021, Romania’s economy grew by 5.6%, after contracting by
3.7% in 2020. Industrial production recorded a signiﬁcant increase
of 7.1%, but the construction sector did not fully recover, reducing
non-essentially by 0.6%. In addition, the increase in world demand
led to an increase in exports by 20.1%, and domestic consumption
boosted imports by 22.1%. The average annual inﬂation rate in
2021 was 5.1%, recording a maximum of 8.2% in December (Table
1.2).
The Russian Federation
The gross domestic product of the Russian Federation increased
by 4.7% in 2021, after a decline of 2.7% in 2020. During 2021,
industrial production increased by an average of 5.3%, and
production in construction by 6.0%.
The increase in external
demand and oil production caused a signiﬁcant increase in
exports (+45.8%), while imports also increased by 26.7%. The
average unemployment rate in 2021 represented 4.8%. Consumer
prices rose by an average of 6.7%, and by the end of the year
inﬂation had risen to 8.4%, amid the side effects of stimulus
policies related to the COVID-19 pandemic, of rising domestic food
prices and the depreciation of the Russian rouble (Table 1.2).


Chapter 1. International context
15
Ukraine
Ukraine’s gross domestic product increased by 3.1% in 2021, after
a decline of 4.0% in 2020.
During the period under review,
industrial production increased by an average of 1.1% and
construction increased by 5.1%. At the same time, exports and
imports increased on average by 34.3% and 33.9%, respectively.
The average annual inﬂation was 9.4%, and in December 2021
was 10.0% (Table 1.2).

Chapter 2
Economy of the Republic of Moldova
2.1
Real sector
Gross domestic product
In 2021, economic activity in the Republic of Moldova accelerated
sharply after the decline of the previous year. This evolution was
mainly supported by domestic demand from the population in the
context of increasing disposable income, a favourable environment
with relatively low interest rates, a rich harvest, but also by the
positive economic activity in the region, which gradually recovered
after the side effects associated with the pandemic. In this way,
the GDP increased by 13.9% compared to 2020. In the ﬁrst quarter,
the GDP recorded an increase of 8.4%, accelerating to 16.5% in
the second quarter of 2021. Subsequently, the annual dynamics
moderated to 11.6% between July and September 2021, after
which it accelerated again up to the value of 18.9% in the fourth
quarter of 2021 compared to the similar period of 2020.
It should be noted that at the beginning of 2022, the NBS
recalculated and revised the GDP for 2020 and 2021 in the
context
of
the
revision
of
macroeconomic
indicators
for
2014-2020, which had a pronounced impact on the dynamics of
GDP from previous periods.
According to the NBS6, the main
premises that caused the need to revise national accounts were
the revision of demographic data according to the new notion of
population with habitual residence, which replaced the notion of
stable population and the revision and updating of statistical and
administrative data sources.
From the perspective of uses (Chart 2.1), the positive evolution of
economic activity in 2021 was mainly determined by the
dynamics of consumption and gross capital formation.
In this
respect, household consumption was 15.5% higher than the
previous year and thus generated an impact of 12.6 percentage
points on GDP dynamics, and gross capital formation generated a
contribution of 5.7 percentage points to GDP dynamics. Exports
increased by 17.5% compared to 2020 and had a positive
contribution of 4.9 percentage points. A positive inﬂuence, but of

By resource categories (Chart 2.2), the GDP growth in 2021 has
been supported by most sectors of the national economy. The
most pronounced positive impact (4.2 percentage points) was
exerted by the dynamics of the agricultural sector. This7, in the
context of favourable agro-meteorological conditions for some
agricultural crops in 2021, increased by 45.0% compared to the
previous year.
At the same time,
a pronounced positive
contribution (3.8 percentage points) was determined by the 14.5%
increase in gross value added in trade8 and 45.9% of value added
in transport and storage, sectors that have recovered after being
severely affected by the previous year’s COVID-19 restrictions.
The information and communications sector increased by 24.3%
compared to 2020 and contributed 1.2 percentage points to GDP
dynamics. The industry dynamics generated a contribution of 1.1
percentage points to the evolution of GDP, mainly in the context
of the increase of the gross added value in the energy industry by
46.6%. The manufacturing industry registered a minor negative
dynamic (-0.2%). The ﬁnancial and insurance activities sector
increased by 9.5% compared to 2020. Positive developments in
2021 were also recorded in the sectors "art, recreation and leisure
activities"
(66.3%),
"health
and
social
assistance"
(8.5%),
"education" (7.9%),
"administrative sector"9
(42.3%) which
together resulted in a contribution of about 1.7 percentage
points10 to the GDP dynamics. Net taxes on products increased by
17.1% and generated a positive impact of 2.3 percentage points
on GDP dynamics in 2021. On the other hand, a negative dynamic
in 2021 is evidenced in the construction sector, whose gross value
added decreased by 16.0%.
Household consumption
In 2021, the ﬁnal consumption of households increased sharply,
being 15.5% higher than in 2020. At the same time, this annual
rate was 23.8 percentage points higher than in 2020 (Chart 2.3).
The
respective
dynamics
of
the
ﬁnal
consumption
of
the
households was mainly determined by the increase of the
expenses for the purchase of services by 19.1% and of the
expenses for the purchase of goods by 13.5%, accordingly.
The positive evolution of the household consumption was
supported by the increase of its disposable income, but also by
the increase of the volume of loans, in the context of relatively
low interest rates during that period.
Thus, the main sources of consumer ﬁnance in 2021 (salary fund,
social beneﬁts, loans, remittances) according to estimates, as a
whole, increased in real terms by about 13.7%, after the severe
decline of previous year (Chart 2.4). This dynamic was largely
determined by the increase in the volume of new loans granted to
individuals and the salary fund.
At the same time, a positive
impact, but of a smaller magnitude, was determined by the
dynamics of remittances and social beneﬁts.

On the other hand, the maintenance of restrictions as well as the
existence of still cautious expectations concerning the further
evolution of the economy in the context of the number of
COVID-19 cases during 2021 can be considered an obstacle in the
consumption dynamics of households during that period.
Investments
In 2021, investments increased by about 24.0% compared to 2020
(Chart 2.5). This dynamic is mainly due to the pronounced impact
from the subcomponent stock variation (22.2 percentage points),
a fact also supported by the rich harvest of 2021. Gross ﬁxed
capital formation increased by only 1.7% and it thus determined a
more modest contribution to the evolution of investments. Within
this, investments in machinery and equipment increased by 6.5%,
and those in construction contracted by 0.7%.
The dynamics of investments in the reference period was
negatively inﬂuenced by the uncertainty related to COVID-19 and
the persistence of restrictions in the activity of economic
operators. At the same time, the overall increase in commodity
quotations and transport costs has affected companies’ results,
disrupting investment plans.
On the other hand, investment
dynamics were supported by the maintenance of relatively low
interest rates.
Analysing the annual rate of investments in ﬁxed assets by
ﬁnancing sources, it is obvious that their evolution has been
supported mainly by investments made from own resources of
enterprises and the population, as well as loans and borrowings
from other sources of ﬁnancing. At the same time, a pronounced
positive impact was determined by the investments ﬁnanced from
the budgets of the administrative-territorial units (Chart 2.6).
Sources from the state budget, as well as the means of foreign
investors recorded a negative dynamic in 2021.
Agricultural production
In 2021, global agricultural production increased by 49.9%
compared to the previous year (Chart 2.7).
The respective
dynamics was determined by the increase of the volume of the
vegetal production by 75.5%. At the same time, the volume of
animal production decreased by 7.0% compared to 2020. Thus,
the largest increases in production volumes were recorded in
wheat crops (2.7 times), corn for grain (3.0 times), rapeseed (by
95.3%), sunﬂower (by 92.9%), sugar beet (by 50.1%) and fruits,
nuts and berries (28.7%). At the same time, the contraction in
production volume in the livestock sector was determined by the
decrease in production for all types of products (meat, milk, and
eggs).
It should be noted that a rich agricultural harvest in 2021, by
overlapping
with
high
prices
on
international
markets
for
agricultural products (wheat, sunﬂower, corn), facilitated the
recovery of the agricultural sector (related to the vegetable
sector) after the severe drought of 2020. On the other hand, the
volume of production in the livestock sector continued the
negative dynamics of previous years.

Freight shipping
In 2021, rail, road, river, and air transport companies transported
19.2% more goods compared to 2020 (Chart 2.9). This increase
was determined by the annual increase in the volume of goods
transported by road (20.8%), rail (15.6%) and air (6.4%). At the
same time, the transport of goods by river decreased in annual
terms by 26.2%.
Passenger transport
In 2021, rail, road, river, and air transport companies carried 9.5%
more passengers compared to 2020 (Chart 2.10). This increase
was determined by the rise in annual terms of the number of
passengers transported by air (2.2 times) and road (9.3%). At the
same time, the number of passengers transported by river and rail
decreased in annual terms by 20.1% and 17.0%, respectively.
Retail trade
In 2021, the volume of turnover of enterprises with retail as
main activity (in comparable prices) increased by 12.0%, this
rate increasing by 7.4 percentage points compared to 2020. At the
same time, the turnover of the enterprises with main activity of
market services provided to the population registered in the same
period a considerable increase in annual terms, of 71.3% (Chart
2.11), rate 87.0 percentage points higher than the previous year.

being 30.1 percentage points higher than in 2020 (Chart 2.12). At

Foreign trade
The
recovery
of
international
and
regional
demand,
by
overlapping with a rich domestic agricultural harvest, created
favourable preconditions for the dynamics of foreign trade of the
Republic of Moldova in 202111. In this sense, the annual rate of
exports in 2021 recorded a level of 27.5%, being 38.7 percentage
points higher than in 2020. The upward dynamics of the annual
rate of exports was determined by the evolution of exports to all
categories of countries (Chart 2.13). It should be noted that the
revival of exports in 2021 was mainly due to the relaxation of
economic and social restrictions imposed at regional and local
level, which aimed to combat the spread of COVID-19 virus. In
2021, the share of exports to the EU represented about 61.1%,
while those to CIS and countries classiﬁed as "Rest of the World"
were 14.8% and 24.1%, respectively.
By product category, the sharp increase in the annual rate of
exports in 2021 was determined by the evolution of exports of
"food, animal products, beverages and fats", "vehicles, optical
appliances and sound recorders and reproducers", "chemical and
wood products" and "articles of metals, stones and ceramics"
(Chart 2.14). The recording of a record agricultural harvest in
2021, by overlapping with the revival of external demand, created
favourable preconditions for the sharp increase in the export of
agri-food products. It should be noted that the automotive industry
worldwide, in 2020, was one of the sectors severely affected by the
COVID-19 pandemic. At the same time, in 2021 there was a revival
of the automotive sector, thus determining the positive dynamics
of exports related to the group "vehicles, optical instruments,
sound recorders and reproducers".
Analysing the evolution of exports by origin of products, the
increase in the annual rate of exports in 2021 was mainly
determined by the increase in exports of domestic goods (Chart
2.15). At the same time, re-exports also recorded a positive but
more modest dynamic.
The annual rate of imports in 2021 was 32.5% or 39.8 percentage
points higher than in 2020. The sharp increase in the annual rate
of imports in 2021 was supported by the evolution of imports from
all major groups of countries (Chart 2.16).

After
the
severe
decline
in
2020,
in
the
context
of
the
COVID-19-related restrictions, in 2021 both the economically
active and employed population registered a positive dynamic.
Thus,
the economically active population constituted 871.6
thousand persons being by 0.5% higher than in 2020, and the
employed population constituted 843.4 thousand, being by 1.1%
higher than in 2020 (Chart 2.18).
At the same time, the activity rate was 41.1%, 0.8 percentage
points higher than in 2020, and the employment rate was 39.8%,

1.0 percentage points higher than in 2020 (Chart 2.19).
At the same time, the number of unemployed in 2021 decreased
by about 4.9 thousand people to the value of 28.2 thousand people.
The unemployment rate was 3.2%, lower (by 0.6 percentage

Salary income
In 2021, the situation on the labour market improved compared
to the previous year. The wage fund in the economy registered
a well-deﬁned positive annual dynamic (16.1%). Adjusted to the
CPI, the wage fund increased by 10.7% compared to 2020. At the
same time, there was an upward trend in the average number of
employees in the national economy, which entered the positive
level of 3.1% (Chart 2.21).
The evolution largely reﬂects the
manifestation of a base effect associated with a signiﬁcant number
of employees going unemployed one year ago, when the pandemic
broke out.
All sectors of the economy recorded increases in the wage fund.
At the same time, the most important positive contributions to
the dynamics of the salary fund come from the sectors health and
social assistance, information and communications, and trade.
On the fundamental factors side, the relaunch of economic
activities has boosted the growth of the wage fund, although
some restrictions related to the COVID-19 pandemic are still valid,
and
some
uncertainties
related
to
the
evolution
of
the
epidemiological situation remain.
In the context of supporting and appreciating the effort of the
medical staff in the ﬁght against the COVID-19 pandemic, the
salary fund for the medical system continued to make the largest
contribution to the positive dynamics of the salary fund. At the
same time, the salary fund in the information and communications
sector, and trade recorded signiﬁcant increases.
During the analysed period, the average earnings in the national
economy recorded an increase of 12.4% compared to 2020. In
the budgetary sector of the economy, there was a more modest
increase in average earnings, of 6.5%, while in the real sector –
14.2%. The salaries of medical staff, which increased in stages
during the year, registered the most signiﬁcant increases. In real
terms, the annual growth rate of average earnings per economy
was 7.0% in 2021 (Chart 2.22), in the real sector – 8.7%, and in
the budgetary sphere – 1.4%.

In 2021, the average annual CPI rate was 5.1%, 1.3 percentage
points higher than in 2020 (Table 2.1). During 2021, the annual
inﬂation rate recorded a sharp upward trajectory. It increased
from 0.4% in December 2020 to 13.9% in December 2021, thus
intersecting, in August 2021, the range of 5.0% ±1.5 percentage
points stipulated in the Medium-term monetary policy strategy
(Chart 2.23).
The acceleration of prices in the above-mentioned period was
determined by the increase in prices for food, oil and other
commodities on the international market, which was reﬂected in
the increase in prices of these products on the local market. First,
in the ﬁrst months of the year, the increase in the annual rate of
fuel prices began, after which, in the second half of the year, the
other subcomponents of the CPI accelerated (Chart 2.25). A sharp
impact was due to the adjustment of tariffs related to regulated
prices in the second half of the previous year, including the
adjustment of gas and heat tariffs in November 2021 in the
context of rising gas prices on the regional market (Chart 2.24). At
the same time, the increase in inﬂation in 2021 was supported by
demand pressures in the context of increasing disposable income
of the population, but also increase in lending.
Inﬂationary
pressures were also exerted by the dynamics of the exchange rate
in 2021. The good harvest of 2021 temporarily delayed, during
the summer months, the spread of the effects of the sharp rise in
food prices observed on the international and regional market.
Later, however, in the last part of the year, with the substitution
of domestic fruits and vegetables with imported ones, food prices
on the domestic market accelerated sharply, generating the
greatest impact on the annual CPI rate.
Core inﬂation12
During 2021, the annual rate of core inﬂation experienced an
upward trajectory.
It increased signiﬁcantly, from 1.6% in
December 2020 to 8.9% in December 2021.
On the side of the fundamental factors, inﬂuences from the
recovery of the ﬁnal consumption of the population were
highlighted. The positive dynamics of the salary fund for both the
real and the budgetary sector, together with the rapid rise in
lending, especially to individuals for new credits for consumption
and real estate, have generated a positive impact on aggregate
demand, leading to inﬂationary pressure on the subcomponents of
core inﬂation. At the same time, the slight depreciation of the
national
currency
against
the
main
reference
currencies
12Core inﬂation is calculated by the National Bureau of Statistics by excluding
prices outside the area inﬂuenced by monetary policy measures promoted by the
National Bank of Moldova, such as food and beverages, fuels, products and services
with regulated prices.


Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
25
supported the increase in the annual rate of core inﬂation. In
2021, the average ofﬁcial exchange rate of the Moldovan leu
depreciated by 2.1% against the US dollar and by 6.0% against
the single European currency.
In the reporting year, the inﬂationary path was marked, mainly, by
the increase of prices for the subcomponents "clothing", "means
of transport, auto parts", "others". And in the segment "recreation
and culture" and "construction materials" we can see a signiﬁcant
increase in prices (Chart 2.26). At the same time, the core inﬂation
continued to receive persistent cost pressures.

Articles of "clothing" and "footwear" became signiﬁcantly more
expensive.
The increase in commodity prices on international
markets has led to higher prices for goods produced or imported
into the country. At the same time, the continuing shortage of
containers around the world has increased the cost of supplying
goods from China.
The "others" subcomponent increased mainly for "telephone and
fax equipment" and "transport services", in particular in November
and December 2021. Taxi services have become more expensive
in the context of gas price adjustments, which also caused the
price increase for 1 cubic meter of methane gas at the ﬁlling
stations.
The "recreation and culture" segment has witnessed signiﬁcant
price increases,
in particular the increase in the price of
international tourist service packages. In the context of limiting
the accommodation capacity of tourist units in order to comply
with the legal norms concerning the security measures related to
the pandemic, they have considerably reduced their activity,
which has led to increased costs.
Additionally, inﬂationary
pressures speciﬁc to the management of the health crisis (costs
related to sanitary materials, disinfectants, etc.), which are felt
more acutely in the areas of activity most exposed to direct
human contact (referring to the services included in the core
inﬂation, it is about the HoReCa sector, cultural and recreational
services).
The important increase in prices for "construction materials",
recorded mainly in the second half of the year under review,
reﬂects the avalanche of their price increases internationally, with
the recovery of economies amid easing restrictive measures
related to COVID-19.
At the same time, the changes operated within the state ﬁscal
policy at the beginning of 2021, concerning the increase of excise
duties, were reﬂected in the increase of cigarette prices.

Food prices
During 2021, the annual rate of food prices recorded a sharp
upward trend, so it increased by 15.8 percentage points, to the
level of 17.5% in December 2021. The upward dynamics of the
annual rate was mainly conditioned by the increase in contributions
from the groups "vegetables", "meat, meat products and canned
meat", "potatoes", "milling and bakery products" and "milk and
dairy products" (Chart 2.27). The sharp rise in international food


26
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
prices, in energy prices overlapping with the revival of aggregate
demand has created considerable inﬂationary pressures on food
prices traded on the local market (pressures fuelled by both costs
and demand).
In the ﬁrst eight months of the year, the annual rate of food prices
remained relatively low, due to the rich agricultural harvest
recorded in 2021. Thus, with the beginning of the process13 of
substituting domestic products with imported ones, we witnessed
a sharp correction in prices and, as a result, a sharp acceleration
in the annual rate of food prices in the last four months of the
year.
It should be noted that in 2021 food prices on the
international market increased by about 30.0% compared to 2020,
thus generating pronounced pro-inﬂationary pressures on the
domestic market.
At the same time, the revival of external aggregate demand has
generated major pressures on commodity prices.
Thus, even
though in 2021 we had a rich harvest of agricultural crops, their
selling price remained at a high level, and for some crops, it was
increasing. In this context, inﬂationary pressures on the "milling
and bakery products", "fats" and "meat and meat products" groups
increased signiﬁcantly, and as a result the prices for these products
in the domestic market were constantly increasing.
It should be mentioned that the global energy crisis recorded in
2021 exerted pronounced inﬂationary pressures on food prices.
Thus, the increase during 2021 of fuel prices by about 30.0%
signiﬁcantly inﬂuenced transportation costs. At the same time, the
substantial adjustment, in November 2021, of the tariff for network
gas also contributed to the increase of costs and, respectively, to
the increase of inﬂationary pressures.
At the same time, the rapid revival of economic activity, after
the easing of restrictions imposed against the background of the
COVID-19 pandemic, contributed to aggregate demand positioning
at a positive level in the second half of 2021. In this context,
the inﬂationary pressures were recorded during the analysed
period from aggregate demand, which, in addition to the above-
mentioned factors, contributed to the increase in inﬂationary
pressures.
Regulated prices
After the ﬁrst ten months of developments close to zero or even
negative values, the annual rate of prices for regulated goods and
services recorded a pronounced evolution in the last two months
of 2021. Thus, in January-October 2021, regulated prices recorded
a value average of -1.8%. Subsequently, in the last two months of
the year, it outlined an upward trajectory, increasing to 13.3% in
December 2021 (Chart 2.28).

Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
27
In this regard, as a result of the estimates made, ANRE found
signiﬁcant positive tariff deviations formed during 2020, which
allowed the initiation of procedures to revise electricity prices and
reduce them. Thus, the ﬁnal consumers served by I.C.S. "Premier
Energy" S.R.L. whose installations of use are connected to the low
voltage electrical distribution networks paid by 9.9% less than the
previous tariff. End consumers served by S.A. "Furnizarea Energiei
Electrice Nord" whose installations of use are connected to the low
voltage distribution networks paid 4.9% less than the old price. It
should be noted that, within the annual rate of regulated prices,
the negative impact of electricity prices persisted until the end of
2021.
At the same time, the negative dynamics of the annual rate of
regulated prices in the ﬁrst half of 2021 was supported by the
effect of reducing the tariff for network gas, according to the
request of S.A. "Moldovagaz" and approved by ANRE on 28 October
202015.
On the other hand, starting with July 2021, the positive dynamics
of
regulated
prices
was
supported
by
the
increase
of
administrative fees (the sub-component "other services" not
highlighted before) by 196.6%. This increase was determined by
the increase by the Public Services Agency16 of the tax for
registration of marriage, issuance of identity card and passport.
Subsequently, more PSA services became cheaper17.
That
decision was taken at the initiative of the PSA, following an
analysis of the costs and resources available. Changes included
marriage
registration,
this
service
becoming
free
for
the
population (the rate before adjustment was MDL 260).
The pronounced upward dynamics of the annual rate of regulated
prices in the last two months of the previous year was mainly
determined by the increase in network gas and district heating
prices by 81.2% and 23.7% respectively,
cumulatively for
November 2021 and December 2021.
In this regard, on 9
November 2021, ANRE, based on the calculations submitted by
S.A. "Moldovagaz" approved a tariff of MDL 10,260/1,000 cubic
meters (without VAT) for natural gas supplied at the points of exit
from the low-pressure natural gas distribution networks, in force
since 1 November 2021.
At the same time, on 20 November 2021, ANRE approved the
provisional prices for the electricity produced and the tariffs for
the heat delivered to consumers, which entered into force on 1
November 202118. Thus, the heat tariff for consumers connected
to the centralized system managed by "Termoelectrica" increased
from MDL 1,122/Gcal to MDL 1,772/Gcal or by 57.9%.
Considering the principle of reﬂecting the actual household
expenditure, the tariff adjustment was partially conveyed by the
NBS, given that the Government decided to compensate a certain
part of the increase. Thus, the Government will compensate the
tariff difference for gas consumption, between November 2021 –

At the end of 2021, major deviations accumulated in several
categories of regulated prices at both the central and local public
administration levels. Over the last 5-10 years, those services
have been directly or indirectly subsidized by local budgets, and
some service providers have accumulated signiﬁcant negative
deviations because of non-adjustment of tariffs to costs. It should
be noted that these inﬂationary pressures may materialize in the
future and will contribute to the evolution of inﬂation both through
the direct effect and through side effects on other goods and
services.
Fuel prices
In 2021, the annual rate of fuel and oil prices was characterized by
a predominantly upward dynamic. Thus, it increased from -6.8%
in January 2021 to the level of 29.3% in December 2021. This
dynamic was supported, in particular, by the increase in prices
of fuel and liqueﬁed gas because of the considerable increase in
oil prices on the international market. Thus, the average price of
Urals oil in 2021 was about 68.8 US dollars/barrel, 65.6% higher
than the average price of oil in 2020 (Chart 2.29). It is worth
mentioning that, on 14 June 2021, during the online public meeting
of the ANRE Board of Directors, the Methodology of calculating
and applying prices to petroleum products (in force from 1 July
2021) was approved20.
At the same time, after the round of
consultations on 1 September 2021, ANRE approved the Decision
on amending some provisions of the Decision of the Board of
Directors of ANRE No. 254 of 14.06.202121. Thus, the wholesale
prices became free, as well as the retail prices of liqueﬁed gas, and
ANRE publishes daily only the maximum retail prices of the main
petroleum products of the standard type – gasoline and diesel. The
upward dynamics of the annual rate of fuel and oil prices in 2021
was also supported by the evolution of liqueﬁed gas prices.
During 2021, wood prices generated more positive contributions
to the evolution of fuel and oil prices compared to 2020. The
upward dynamics of coal prices on the international market and
the uncertainty of the import price of natural gas supported the
increase in prices of coal and wood on the domestic market in the
latter part of the year.

Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
29
Industrial prices
In the ﬁrst ten months of 2021, the annual rate of prices in industry
recorded a slightly upward trajectory, and in the last two months
of the year it accelerated sharply. Thus, in December 2021 the
annual rate of prices in industry represented 16.1%, being 12.3
percentage points higher than in December 2020. It should be
noted that during 2021 the annual rate of prices for products
delivered on the domestic market positioned mainly at a higher
level than that related to prices for products delivered on the
foreign market (Chart 2.30).
Analysing the evolution of the annual rate of prices in industry
by main branches, it is clear that, as in previous periods, the
major contribution to the formation of the annual rate is the
manufacturing industry (Chart 2.31). It should be noted that the
annual rate of prices in the manufacturing industry was relatively
stable in the ﬁrst ten months of 2021, and, in November and
December 2021, it visibly increased. The increase in the annual
rate of prices in the manufacturing industry during 2021 was
largely due to higher prices in the food industry, the manufacture
of beverages, textiles, other non-metallic mineral products and
in the production of furniture and wood processing.
Prices in
the energy sector increased signiﬁcantly in the last two months
of 2021, thus registering in December an annual rate of 63.8%.
At the same time, the prices in the extractive industry recorded
more modest growth rates, and the impact on industry prices was
negligible.

Prices in construction
During 2021, the annual rate of construction prices recorded an
upward trajectory. Thus, in the fourth quarter of 2021 it stood
at 4.3%, being 3.4 percentage points higher than in the fourth
quarter of 2020 (Chart 2.32).
Producer’s prices for agricultural products
In 2021, producer prices for agricultural products increased by
13.7% compared to the previous year.
This development was
determined by the increase in prices for animal and vegetable
products by 20.4% and 12.6%, respectively (Chart 2.33).
The evolution of prices for vegetable products was mainly
determined by the increase in prices for "soybean" (45.3%),
"rapeseed" (41.0%), "sunﬂower" (25.3%), "corn" (13.0%) and
"wheat" (10.2%). It should be noted that in 2021 the agricultural
harvest was very good for most products, and their marketing
prices increased compared to those of 202022. The rise in prices
for most product groups was determined by international prices,
which rose against the background of a revival of global economic
activity.
22Against the background of the pronounced drought recorded in 2020, the
agricultural harvest was a modest one, and the prices for vegetable products
increased signiﬁcantly.


30
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
The increase in prices for animal products was determined by the
rise in prices for all product categories. Thus, the prices for eggs,
meat (live weight) and milk increased by 29.0%, 20.6% and 5.6%,
respectively. It should be noted that the decrease in the volume of
animal production for the ﬁfth consecutive year has contributed
to the steady decrease in the supply of animal products on the
domestic market. Consequently, the decrease in the supply of
animal products by overlapping with the increase in production
costs has inﬂuenced the upward dynamics of producer prices for
animal products.


Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
31
2.3
Public sector and tax policy
Implementing the national public budget
In 2021, the indicators of the national public budget were affected
by the risks and uncertainties caused by the developments in the
COVID-19 pandemic situation. As a result, public revenues and
expenditures increased at a faster pace, the budget deﬁcit
amounted to MDL 4,640.5 million.
The recovery of economic
activity, the relaunch of domestic demand and the increase of
imports supported the incomes of the population and of the
economic operators and respectively the taxes and duties
collected to the state budget.
Thus, according to the data

provided by the Ministry of Finance, the revenues accumulated to
the national public budget constituted MDL 77,373.1 million, by
23.5% above the level of revenues accumulated in 2020 (Chart
2.34).
Compared to GDP data, revenues collected in 2021
amounted to 32.0%, 0.6 percentage points more than the
previous year.
Most of the budget revenues was accumulated from taxes and fees,
which had a share of 63.8% (MDL 49,388.8 million). Compulsory
insurance contributions and premiums amounted to 28.3% (MDL
21,839.1 million), and the share of other income and grants was
4.8% (MDL 3,697.8 million) and 3.2%, respectively (MDL 2,447.4
million) from the total budget revenues.
During the reporting year, the Republic of Moldova received
budget ﬁnancial support in the form of external grants amounting
to MDL 2,447.4 million or 3.8 times more than in 2020. In relation
to GDP, the grants constituted 1.0%, 0.7 percentage points more
than in 2020.
Assistance provided by international ﬁnancial
organizations and donor countries is an important contribution to
economic recovery and implementation of reforms necessary for
the modernization and relaunch of the Republic of Moldova.
At the same time, budget expenditures have been increased to
ensure public health, improve the effects of the pandemic and
support the population.
As a result, the expenditures of the
national public budget totalled in the reference year MDL 82,013.6
million, registering an increase of 11.9% compared to 2020. Of
total expenditures, the largest share concerns expenditures for
social protection (35.9%), for health care 16.2%, and for education
13.3%. Public spending, relative to GDP, amounted to 33.9% or
2.8 percentage points less than in 2020.
It should be mentioned that, during 2021, the amount of MDL
31,168.8 million was transferred from the state budget (SB) to
the state social insurance budget (SSIB), the Mandatory Health
Insurance Fund (MHIF) and the local budgets (LB),(increasing by
15.8% compared to 2020). Most transfers from the state budget
were directed to local budgets, about 46.9% of total transfers,
followed by transfers to SSIB, about 34.4%, the MHIF receiving a
more modest funding of 18.7%.
In this context, in 2021, the implementation of the national public
budget resulted in a deﬁcit of MDL 4,640.5 million. Relative to


32
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
GDP, this deﬁcit represents 1.9%, with 3.4 percentage points below
the level of 2020. The sources of ﬁnancing the national public
deﬁcit consisted of net external loans amounting to MDL 8,299.6
million, which represents 3.4% of the GDP (decreasing by 0.2
percentage points compared to the previous year), from domestic
debts and loans in the amount of MDL 3,772.7 million or 1.6%
of GDP, decreasing by 1.2 percentage points compared to the
previous year). At the same time, the balances of funds in the
accounts of the component budgets of the national public budget
at the end of the year amounted to MDL 11,868.3 million.

incomes and services increased.
Deﬁcit of foreign trade in goods24 recorded in the balance of
payments amounted to USD 4,190.4 million, increasing by 35.4%
due to the more pronounced increase in imports of goods (USD
+1,713.9 million) than exports (USD +617.8 million). The ratio
between the deﬁcit balance of foreign trade in goods and GDP was
-30.6% (compared to -26.8% in 2020).
The export of goods recorded in the balance of payments
(excluding goods after/for processing) totalled USD 2,562.3 million
(+31.8% compared to 2020). In 2021, both the physical volume of
exports of goods and their value increased considerably, including
due to the increase in prices for exported goods.
The geographical structure of exports of goods in 2021 shows that
55.8% were directed to EU-27 countries25, 18.6% were directed
to CIS countries, and the remaining 25.6% of exports went to
other countries. Exports of goods to all 3 regions increased, and
signiﬁcant contributions were made to exports to the category
"other countries" which increased by 73.4% compared to 2020
and to EU-27 countries – by 21.7%, being followed by those to the
CIS countries which increased by 23.4% (Chart 2.36).
From the point of view of the structure of exports by categories
of goods, the increase was determined by the category "agri-food
products", which exports increased by 32.0% compared to 2020,
mainly due to the increase in exports of vegetable products (wheat
and meslin, corn, rapeseed, etc.). Agri-food products continued
to predominate in the structure of exports of goods with a share
of 56.2% of the total. Exports of base metals and articles thereof
also increased (+2.7 times or up to USD 133.6 million compared
to USD 49.0 million in 2020), machinery and appliances (+12.9%

the increase in imports of goods was made by EU-27 countries,
which increased by 30.6%, followed by imports from CIS countries,
which increased by 41.8%, and those from other countries – by
31.4%. Thus, in the geographical structure of imports of goods, in
2021, the EU-27 accounted for 51.3% of the total, CIS – 29.8%,
other countries – 18.9% (Chart 2.37).
From the point of view of the structure of imports by categories
of goods, the increase was determined by the category "mineral
products", which imports increased by 78.7% compared to 2020
(due to the increase in oil prices), as well as the import of machines
and appliances by 28.3%, agri-food products – by 19.5% more than
in 2020.
Import of energy products and electricity, in 2021, amounted to
USD 1,037.4 million, an increase of 85.4% compared to 2020, due
to the increase in prices on international markets. Thus, the most
signiﬁcant increase was the import of natural gas (in gaseous
state) – 2.3 times, diesel fuel by 65.6%, gasoline – by 75.0%, coal
– by 16.6%, while electricity imports decreased by 19.0% (Chart
2.38).
The surplus of foreign trade in services increased by 19.7%
compared to 2020, to USD 457.5 million. This development was
due to a more signiﬁcant increase in services exports (USD
+356.9 million) than imports (USD +281.7 million).
The ratio between services balance and GDP was 3.3%, remaining
at 2020 level (Chart 2.39). In exports, travel services made the
largest positive contribution.
Among travel services, personal
travel increased by 32.5% and business travel by 31.5%. Also,
there was a signiﬁcant increase (+35.5%) in the export of IT
services, as well as transport services, which rose by 32.2%
compared to 2020.
The increase in imports of services resulted from the increase in
imports of transport services (+38.5%, especially freight transport,
which increased by 35.3%) and travel (+34.4% compared to 2020).
The surplus of primary income decreased by 3.5% against 2020
to USD 376.9 million, amounting to 2.8% of GDP (Chart 2.40).
This decrease was due to a more signiﬁcant increase in outﬂows
(USD +42.4 million) than inﬂows (USD +28.9 million). Thus, the
remuneration of resident employees for the work performed for
non-resident employers increased by 5.4% to USD 863.8 million
and, at the same time, payments from direct investment income
increased (+12.8%).
Secondary income recorded a surplus increase by 23.6% against
2020 to USD 1,765.8 million (12.9% of GDP) (Chart 2.41). Personal
transfers received from abroad by Moldovan residents increased
by 15.5% and amounted to USD 1,220.8 million. The net amount
of technical assistance, humanitarian aid, and grants received
under international cooperation by all institutional sectors virtually
doubled in 2021 to USD 349.0 million.

Republic of Moldova in 2021 amounted to USD 2,028.1 million
(up 12.0% compared to 2020), which represents 14.8% relative to
GDP (-0.9 percentage points compared to 2020). The increase in
remittance inﬂows was driven by higher inﬂows from EU-27 and
other countries (+23.2% and +10.0%, respectively) (Chart 2.42).
At the same time, remittances from CIS countries decreased (by
13.8% compared to 2020), both due to the restriction of seasonal
workers’ travel and depreciation of the Russian rouble. At the same
time, remittances to non-residents (debit) amounted to USD 427.7
million, increasing by 30.3%.
Capital account recorded a deﬁcit of USD 51.0 million, which
decreased by 22.4% compared to 2020.
The net inﬂows of
external assistance received by the public administration to
ﬁnance investment projects amounted to USD 24.7 million
(increasing by 2.1 times compared to 2020), while net capital
outﬂows by the private sector fell by 2.0% to USD 75.7 million.
The amount of the current account and capital account balances
indicates that, as a result of the current and capital transactions
of the residents of the Republic of Moldova with non-residents, the
external net ﬁnancing requirement amounted to USD 1,641.1
million, which represents 12.0% related to GDP (compared to 8.3%
in 2020).
In 2021, ﬁnancial account resulted in net capital inﬂows of USD
1,602.6 million due to a net increase in residents’ liabilities to non-
residents by USD 1,147.1 million generated by actual transactions,
as well as the net decrease in residents’ external ﬁnancial assets
by USD 455.4 million (Chart 2.43).
Liabilities increased, in particular, due to commitments in the
form of trade credits and advances, amounting to USD 360.5
million, received from non-resident trading partners, as well as
net withdrawals of 301.2 million USD recorded on external loans
(withdrawals – USD 827.0 million, repayments – USD 525.8 million)
(table A.2).
The public administration contracted in 2021 new external loans
amounting to USD 378.8 million, the creditors being: International
Monetary Fund – USD 79.8 million, International Development
Association – USD 76.4 million, European Investment Bank – USD
61.7 million, European Commission – 57.7 million USD, Council of
Europe Development Bank – USD 44.4 million, European Bank for
Reconstruction and Development – USD 41.3 million, International
Bank for Reconstruction and Development – USD 12.8 million,
International Fund for Agricultural Development – USD 4.3 million,
and
other
creditors.
Reimbursements
made
by
general
government during the same period amounted to USD 141.2
million.
The National Bank of Moldova reimbursed USD 45.6
million of previously borrowed loans from the IMF. Licensed banks
made net withdrawals for USD 45.1 million, and the other sectors
– net withdrawals of USD 64.1 million.

increased by USD 244.8 million during 2021, driven by the

The international investment position (IIP) of the Republic
of Moldova, on 31 December 2021, amounted to USD -5,661.0
million (or 41.4% of GDP), the net debt balance deepening by 4.3%
compared to the end of 2020 (Table A.3, chart 2.45).
The external ﬁnancial assets position amounted to USD 6,266.4
million, up 7.1% from the beginning of the year, and liabilities to
USD 11,927.4 million, up 5.7%.

The increase in the position of ﬁnancial assets was due to other
changes (USD +1,019.0 million). At the same time, the negative
ﬂow in the balance of payments (USD -455.4 million), the
ﬂuctuation of the exchange rates of the original currencies against
the US dollar (USD -147.4 million) and the change in the prices of
ﬁnancial instruments (USD -1.8 million) inﬂuenced downward the
position of ﬁnancial assets.
From the point of view of the structure of PII by functional
categories, the increase in the position of external ﬁnancial
assets during 2021 was determined by the increase by 17.4% of
the position of assets in the form of other investments , by 3.1%
of the position of reserve assets and by 1.5% of those in the form
of direct investments. At the same time, asset positions in the
form of portfolio investments and ﬁnancial derivatives decreased
by 44.9%.
The position of ofﬁcial reserve assets also increased (+3.1%) and
amounted to USD 3,901.9 million at 31.12.2021, which meets all
sufﬁciency criteria: coverage of at least 3 months of imports of
goods and services (covered 5.9 months of actual import); full
coverage of short-term external debt by reserves (they covered
142.2%); coverage of 20% of the M2 money supply (they covered
83.3%); coverage of 100-150% of the amount: 30% of short-term
external debt +15% of other external commitments +5% of M2
+5% of exports of goods and services (they covered 199.2%)
(Chart2.46).

increase by 9.2% of the commitments in the form of other
investments
and
by
1.1%
of
those
in
the
form
of
direct
investments (Chart 2.47).
The position of commitments in the form of other investments
increased, in particular, due to the 13.9% increase in trade credits
and advances in other sectors, as well as the 2.3 times increase in
the special drawing rights position, due to the balance of payments
ﬂow (USD +234.5 million), which represents SDR allocations by
the IMF as support in combating the global economic crisis.
The position of commitments in the form of direct investments has
increased by 1.1% since the beginning of 2021. The position of
direct investments in equity totalled USD 2,884.9 million (+2.6%
compared to 31.12.2020), and of debt instruments – USD 1,915.9
million (-1.0%).
In the geographical distribution of direct
investments in the form of participations and accumulated shares,
investors from EU-27 countries accounted for the majority share
of 85.9%, those from the CIS held a share of 0.8% of total equity
accumulated, and those from other countries – 13.3%.

public

Public debt
At the end of 2021, the public debt of the Republic of Moldova
amounted to MDL 77,752.7 million, representing 32.2% of the GDP.
The public debt increased, at the end of 2021, by MDL 9,932.0
million or by 14.6%, due to the increase of the external public debt
recalculated in the national currency (with a positive contribution of
8.7 percentage points) and of internal public debt (with a positive
contribution of 5.9 percentage points). As a share of GDP, the
internal state debt amounted to 13.8%, and the external debt
18.4% (Chart 2.49).

As of 31 December 2021, the public debt was formed by the
external public debt in proportion of 57.2% and the internal public
debt in proportion of 42.8%.
The external public debt balance, in foreign currency, as of 31
December 2021, increased by 11.8% compared to 31 December
2020 and amounted to USD 2,506.1 million. The external public
debt, in MDL, totalled at the end of the reporting year MDL 44,471.3
million, a level higher than at the end of 2020 by 15.3%.
The internal public debt, at the end of 2021, registered MDL
33,281.4 million, higher than the one at the end of 2020 by 13.8%.
The increase in internal public debt was due to additional SS issues
on the primary market by 29.0%. It should be mentioned that in
2021 the SS issued on 4 October 2016 were redeemed for the
execution of payment obligations derived from state guarantees,
for MDL 260.0 million. Therefore, at the end of 2021, the internal
debt consisted of SS issued on the primary market (56.4%), SS
issued for execution of state guarantees (37.1%) and converted
SS (6.5%).
In 2021, for the service of the public debt, funds in amount of
MDL 1,897.3 million from the national public budget were used
(by 12.7% more compared to 2020). The service of external public
debt constituted MDL 378.0 million (by 4.6% less compared to
2020), and the service of internal public debt constituted MDL
1,519.3 million (by 14.9% more compared to 2020).

during 2021 by 6.8% and totalled USD 9,013.7 million as of 31

compared to 31 December 2020, due to GDP growth) (Chart 2.50,
table A.4).
In terms of maturity structure (original maturity), the increase in
external debt during 2021 was determined by the increase in long-
term debt by 4.3%, compared to the situation of 31 December

Government debt to the National Bank
As of 31 December 2021, the public debt to the National Bank in
the form of state securities in the NBM’s portfolio totalled MDL
14,414.6 million at face value and consisted of:
• government bonds (GB) issued and transmitted to the
National Bank on 4 October 2016 to execute the obligations
of the Ministry of Finance derived from state guarantees No.
807 of 17 November 2014 and No. 101 of 1 April 2015 – MDL
12,351.2 million.
They have an interest rate (coupon) of

10-25 years;
• state securities obtained in the NBM portfolio following the
conversion of loans previously contracted by the Ministry of

obtained in the NBM’s own portfolio because of reissues had
a maturity up to one year (T-bills) with an average interest
rate of 6.54%.
In 2021, the state securities issued for the execution of payment
obligations derived from state guarantees were redeemed, for MDL
260.0 million.

As of 31 December 2021, the balance of term deposits of the
Ministry of Finance was null.

December 2021.
On 31 December 2021, the balance of the Treasury Single Account
of the Ministry of Finance opened with the National Bank was
