ﬁnancial resources in foreign currency payable on demand in the
equivalent of MDL 7,681.85 million (USD 155.48 million and EUR
244.98 million).


41
Chapter 3
Monetary Policy
3.1
Monetary policy objective
In accordance with the provisions of Law on the National Bank of
Moldova No.548-XIII of 21 July 1995, the NBM is the central bank of
the Republic of Moldova, being an autonomous public legal entity
that establishes and promotes monetary and foreign exchange
policy in the state. During 2021, the NBM operated in accordance
with the Medium-term monetary policy strategy, approved by the
Council of Administration of the National Bank of Moldova on 27
December 2012.
According to the Strategy, to ensure and maintain price stability,
the NBM set the continuous inﬂation target, measured by the
consumer price index, published monthly by the National Bureau
of Statistics (NBS), at the level of 5.0% with a possible deviation of
±1.5 percentage points.
During 2021, the National Bank of Moldova promoted a monetary
policy of gradual tightening, aiming to mitigate the inﬂationary
pressures generated by the second-round effects of global price
increases, production and distribution costs of energy resources
and food products.
3.2
Monetary policy decisions
The conduct of monetary policy during 2021 can be divided into
two distinctive periods. In the ﬁrst period, from the beginning
of the year to the end of July, monetary policy decisions were
aimed at mitigating the impact of the pandemic on economic
activity by creating appropriate monetary conditions that would
contribute to bringing inﬂation back within the variation range
of ±1.5 percentage points from the target of 5.0%, seeking to
Chart
3.1:
The
evolution
of
monetary
policy
instruments (%)
Source: NBM
maintain sufﬁcient liquidity, and decrease the cost of favourable
bank credits to support domestic demand, including lending, as
well as stimulating consumption and investments.
Thus, in the ﬁrst part of 2021, the National Bank of Moldova
continued to take measures for easing the monetary policy started
during the previous year.


42
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
During this period, the ratio of required reserves from the funds
attracted in Moldovan lei was reduced during 2021 to 26.0%.
The decisions focused on increasing the available liquidity for
licensed banks, to prevent liquidity risk and strengthen the stability
of the banking sector. At the same time, the reduction of the
required reserve ratio aimed to support the relaunch of economic
activity, aiming to maintain the upward trend of lending activity
to the real economy, reduce the cost of bank loans and further
stimulate the use of instruments in the national currency to the
detriment of those in foreign currency.
The second half of 2021 was inﬂuenced by the ampliﬁcation of
risks and uncertainties at the global, regional, and national level,
generated by the recovery after the COVID-19 pandemic crisis,
and uncertainty associated with the global energy crisis, while
the prospects for economic conditions in the context of these
events were precarious.
In most countries, domestic demand
has recovered at a faster pace than supply and has led to an
increase in producer and consumer prices to their highest levels
in decades.
In such circumstances, central banks have taken
measures to tighten monetary conditions to the extent of slowing
down price growth, but not affecting the still fragile economic
recovery. Thus, in the second part of the year, the NBM operated
measures to gradually tighten the monetary policy with the aim
of alleviating the inﬂationary pressures generated by the second-
round effects of the increase in global prices, production and
distribution costs of energy resources and food products. At the
same time, the inﬂationary pressures were also generated by
the internal aggregate demand supported by the increase in the
population’s income (salary fund, social assistance, remittances
etc.) and the increase in the volume of consumer and mortgage
loans.
In this regard, the NBM Executive Board decided to gradually
increase the monetary policy interest rate from 2.65% to 6.50%
annually (Chart 3.1).
The increase in the base rate aimed at
creating the monetary conditions necessary to temper the growth
rates of consumer prices.


Chapter 3. Monetary Policy
43
3.3
Monetary policy achievements
Money market operations, standing facilities, and the required
reserve
mechanism
continued
to
play
a
key
role
in
the
implementation of monetary policy. The NBM used intensively and
in a ﬂexible manner both the instruments intended to absorb
excess liquidity from the market and those intended to provide
liquidity to banks, resorting in both positions to money market
operations and standing facilities offered to banks. The required
reserve ratio related to the banks’ liabilities in Moldovan lei was
reduced cumulatively by 6.00 percentage points, while that
related to the required reserves applicable to the banks’ liabilities
in foreign currency was maintained at the level of the previous
year.
Open market operations
Issuance of NBM certiﬁcates
The NBM’s interventions as a seller on the foreign exchange market
in a net volume of USD 305.4 million (about MDL 5,410.7 million),
ampliﬁed by the expansionary impact of the operations of the
Ministry of Finance on the money market, but partially mitigated
by the reduction of the ratio of required reserves in Moldovan lei,
affected the volume of operations intended for liquidity absorption,
which decreased by about 1/3 compared to the one recorded in
2020. The situation was also reﬂected in the annual average of
Chart 3.2: The evolution of daily balance of open
market operations (MDL million)
Source: NBM
the stock of NBC placements, which decreased by MDL 1,395.6
million and registered MDL 4,525.5 million (Chart 3.2).
The daily balance of the NBC reached a peak in January, with a
maximum of MDL 7,063.6 million, and gradually decreased until
the end of the year, registering the minimum value at the
beginning of December,
MDL
1,821.2 million,
due to the
consistent sales of foreign currency by the NBM between August
and December 2021. The balance of NBC placements at the end
of 2021 constituted MDL 3,701.3 million at the selling price.
The NBM conducted weekly auctions for placing NBCs with a 14-
day maturity and maximum interest rate equivalent to the NBM’s
base rate.
For the whole of 2021, the weighted average rate
of NBC placed amounted to 3.41%, compared to 3.56% in the
previous year.
Repo operations
The National Bank continued to run repo operations throughout
2021,
providing
additional
support
to
banks
in
liquidity
management.
These were carried out at the initiative of the
National Bank on a weekly basis, through the procedure of
auctions without a ceiling, according to the calendar of monetary
policy operations. Thus, the NBM ran 14-day repo auctions at a
ﬁxed rate (base rate plus a margin of 0.25 percentage points).
The reduction in the level of liquidity on the money market was
associated
with
an
intensiﬁcation
of
banks’
call
for
repo
operations, concentrated in the second half of the year. Thus, the
volume of liquidity delivered (MDL 1,387.0 million) was clearly
higher than the previous year (MDL 181.0 million).


44
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Standing facilities
The interest rate corridor related to standing facilities, used by
the NBM during 2021, further signalled the general orientation of
the monetary policy, contributing to the maintenance of interest
rates on the money market mainly within the limits of the rate
corridor, since the close relationship between the monetary policy
rate and market interest rates is an important factor in monetary
transmission.
Interest rates on standing facilities stagnated in the ﬁrst half of
2021, then followed an upward trend consisting of four increases,
accompanied by a narrowing of the corridor in the second part
of the year.
Thus, in July and September, the National Bank
increased the interest rates on overnight loans and deposits by
one percentage point each, up to 7.15% and 2.15%, respectively.
At its extraordinary meeting of 5 October 2021, the NBM Executive
Board, continuing the tightening of monetary policy, in addition to
the classic increase in interest rates, also opted for an additional
measure – the reduction of standing facilities corridor by 0.50
percentage points, from +/-2.50% to +/-2.00%, while maintaining
its symmetry. The interest rate on credit facility was increased by
0.35 percentage points to 7.50%, and for overnight deposits by
1.35 percentage points to 3.50%.
In December 2021, interest rates were again increased by 1.00
percentage points, so that at the end of 2021 they reached 8.50%
on overnight loans and 4.50% on overnight deposits.
During 2021, banks used both standing facilities, mainly at the
end of the periods of application of required reserves.
Overnight deposit facility
The use of the deposit facility throughout the year to absorb the
net surplus in the banking system implied a better capacity of the
banks to manage short-term funds.
Banks’ recourse to the deposit facility offered by the NBM, although
decreasing compared to the previous year, remained consistent,
especially by the end of the reserve requirement periods, as banks
fulﬁlled their obligation to maintain required reserves.
The total volume of overnight deposits placed by banks with the
National Bank in 2021 amounted to MDL 59.3 billion (2020: MDL
73.37 billion), which indicates an average daily balance of MDL
219.1 million (2020: MDL 262.6 million).
Chart 3.3: The evolution of average monthly balance
of overnight deposits and of average interest rate
Source: NBM
The average monthly balance of banks’ overnight placements
recorded uneven values throughout 2021, with the maximum
(MDL 347.8 million) being recorded in January, and the minimum
(MDL 93.9 million) in July (Chart 3.3).
Overnight credit facility
The credit facility was requested, particularly, at the end of reserve
requirement periods by a limited number of banks, to comply with
the reserve requirement regime.
The total amount of overnight credits granted by the NBM in 2021
amounted to MDL 2,191.5 million, registering a signiﬁcant increase
compared to the previous year (MDL 326.0 million).


Chapter 3. Monetary Policy
45
Required reserves
At the beginning of 2021, to set the monetary conditions that would
contribute to bringing inﬂation back within the variation range of
± 1.5 percentage points from the 5.0% target, the NBM Executive
Board continued the incentive monetary policy measures adopted
in the second half of the previous year.
The decisions to reduce the ratio of required reserves from the
funds attracted in Moldovan lei and in non-convertible currency
Chart 3.4: The evolution of required reserves in MDL
and in foreign currency in 2019-2021 (%)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.5: The evolution of the attracted means subject
to reservation in the years 2019-2021 (averages by
observation periods)
Source: NBM
were adopted to support the relaunch of economic activity, aiming
to maintain adequate liquidity and, particularly, the upward trend
of the lending activity of the real economy, ensuring the sufﬁciency
of liquidity required for this purpose (Chart 3.4). Thus, the ratio
of required reserves in Moldovan lei was reduced from 32.0% to
26.0%, and that in foreign currency was maintained at 30.0%,
as the banking sector continued to have an excess of foreign
exchange liquidity as well. The decisions were aimed at mitigating
the risks associated with a negative ﬁscal impulse and consistent
with the strategic goal of balancing the conditions of ﬁnancial
intermediation in national currency and foreign currency.
In this regard, the required reserves in Moldovan lei maintained by
banks between 16 December 2021 and 15 January 2022 amounted
to MDL 12,514.6 million, down by 8.5% compared to the reserves
maintained by banks in the period from 16 December 2020 to
15 January 2021, with the funds attracted in Moldovan lei and
non-convertible currencies (NCC) of which those are composed
recording an increase of 12.6% (Chart 3.5).
As of 31 December 2021, required reserves in freely convertible
currencies (FCC) held by banks amounted to USD 144.5 million
and EUR 404.2 million. Compared to 31 December 2020, required
reserves in US dollars increased by 1.5%, and EUR – by 24.4%,
against the background of the increase in the attracted funds
subject to the reserve requirement in the respective currencies by
approximately 1.6% and 24.5%, respectively.
According to art.17 of Law No. 548/1995 on the National Bank of
Moldova, required reserves may be remunerated, and, according
to the relevant regulatory framework, if the required reserves are
remunerated, interest is paid on the part exceeding 5% of the
liabilities based on which the required reserves are constituted.
Respectively, in 2021, the NBM’s interest expenses for required
reserves amounted to MDL 132.4 million, an increase of about
40.0% compared to 2020. At the same time, the rate applied to
the NBM’s overnight deposit facility, from which required reserves
in MDL are remunerated, rose from 0.15% to 4.44% at the end of
the reporting year.


46
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Intervention on the domestic foreign exchange market
In 2021, the National Bank of Moldova intervened on the domestic
foreign exchange market both as buyer and seller of foreign
currency, for monetary policy purposes, to avoid excessive
ﬂuctuations in the exchange rate of the Moldovan leu in the
context of signiﬁcant foreign exchange surpluses or deﬁcits on the
interbank market at different times of the year.
During the analysed period, the volume of net sales interventions
of the National Bank of Moldova carried out on the interbank
Chart 3.6: The evolution of the ofﬁcial exchange rate
of MDL/USD and the volume of daily transactions of the
NBM in 2019-2021
Source: NBM
foreign exchange market against Moldovan lei amounted to USD
305.38 million. Additionally, currency conversions were made with
World Bank institutions in the form of net procurements of USD
4.64 million (Chart 3.6).
At the same time, during the reference period, the NBM conducted
purchase swap transactions worth USD 36.46 million (of which
USD 28.46 million equivalent of EUR 25.00 million).


Chapter 3. Monetary Policy
47
3.4
Monetary
and
foreign
exchange
conditions
Dynamic of monetary indicators
The effect of the pandemic crisis was also reﬂected in the
evolution of monetary indicators, which, during 2021, recorded
strong increases. Thus, the annual growth rate of broad money
supply at the end of 2021 was 11.3% in annual terms.
The
behaviour of all monetary aggregates during the reference year
was similar.
Chart 3.7: Evolution of the components of money
supply M2 (%, increase from previous year)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.8: Dynamics of the main components of money
supply M3 (%, increase from previous year)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.9:
The balance of loans granted in the
economy (%, increase at the end of the year)
Source: NBM
Money supply
Throughout 2021, the COVID-19 pandemic continuously affected
the economic activity nationally and worldwide. At the same time,
the faster recovery of the world economy and of the main foreign
trade partners of the Republic of Moldova, together with the
inﬂationary pressures supported by the domestic aggregate
demand,
which was fuelled by the recovery of population
consumption against the background of the increase in the wage
bill in the economy, and of the volume of new credits granted and
transfers of funds in favour of individuals, inﬂuenced the money
market of the Republic of Moldova. As a result, the money supply
(M2) increased by 9.5% in 2021, being 10.7 percentage points
below the level of annual growth in 2020 (Chart 3.7).
A more detailed analysis of the M2 monetary aggregate reﬂects
the maintenance of the growth rate around 20.0% annually until
the third quarter of 2021, after which, in the fourth quarter of 2021,
being also inﬂuenced by the base effect, the growth rate of the
components of the M2 monetary aggregate slowed down. Both
deposits in Moldovan lei and "money in circulation" component
recorded the same behaviour during 2021. Thus, at the end of
2021, deposits in the national currency and money in circulation
increased against 2020 by 11.5% and 6.3%, respectively.
The evolution of deposits in Moldovan lei was largely determined
by the increase in sight deposits of both individuals and legal
entities, which at the end of December reached 14.3%. The term
deposits made a positive contribution, their increase being 8.0%.
At the end of December 2021, money in circulation totalled MDL
31.7 billion, increasing by MDL 1.9 billion since the beginning of
the year. The annual growth rate of "money in circulation" by the
end of 2021 slowed to 6.3% annually. During the year, there was
an increase on average in cash turnovers compared to the previous
year. The most signiﬁcant contributions to the growth of cash in
circulation came from net ﬂows from operations with current and
deposit accounts, net ﬂows from operations with foreign currency
from individuals and payments on loans to individuals.
At the same time, the (M3) money supply recorded an annual
growth of 11.3%, the rate of increase being lower than in 2020 by
8.4 percentage points (Chart 3.8).

Annual Report (NBM, 2021)

At the end of 2021, the growth rate of foreign currency deposits
was 15.6%, being determined by the major contribution of sight

registered an increase of 4.7% annually, while demand deposits
increased by 25.9%.
At the end of 2021, the share of deposits in MDL in total deposits
was 58.3% and the share of foreign currency deposits – 41.7%,

In the structure of deposits in MDL, the share of term deposits in

Lending market
At the end of 2021, the annual growth rate of the total balance
of loans granted in the economy by licensed banks was 21.0%,
higher than at the end of 2020 by 10.7 percentage points (Chart
3.9). The annual dynamics of the balance of loans granted in the
economy was largely determined by the acceleration of the growth
of the component of loans granted to individuals in MDL, which
share at the end of the year was 40.1%, higher than at the end
of 2020 by 5.7 percentage points. At the end of 2021, the annual
growth rate of the balance of loans granted to individuals in MDL
increased by 24.6 percentage points compared to that at the end
of 2020 and constituted 41.0%. The increase was determined
by the accelerated evolution of new loans granted in MDL, in the
context of the downward trend of interest rates.
At the end of the reporting year, the balance of loans granted to
legal entities, which share was 59.1%, recorded a growth rate of
10.8%, higher than at the end of 2020 by 3.6 percentage points.
The increase was caused by the rise in the balance of loans granted
both in national currency and foreign currency recalculated in MDL.
The balance of national currency loans granted in the economy
recorded a double annual growth rate compared to the end of
2020 (30.7%, compared to 14.1% at the end of 2020) (Chart 3.10).
The share of the balance of loans granted in the economy in the
national currency constituted 69.0% and increased by about 5
percentage points. It should be noted that the growth rate of the
balance of loans granted directly in foreign currency decreased
compared to the end of 2020.
Evolution of new loans granted by the licensed banks
The annual dynamics of new loans granted by licensed banks
reﬂected, during 2021, the revival of lending, stimulated by the
downward trend of interest rates among other things. The annual
increase in new loans granted was signiﬁcantly inﬂuenced by the
evolution of the component in the national currency.

In 2021, the total volume of new loans granted by licensed banks
in the national currency had an upward trend and amounted to
MDL 32,584.6 million, higher than in 2020 by 41.8% (Chart 3.11).
As a result of this evolution, the share of new loans granted in
MDL in total loans increased to 72.4%. The advance occurred
due to the signiﬁcant growth in loans to individuals, even against
pre-pandemic levels. The share of the component of loans granted
to individuals increased to 48.7% in 2021, compared to 40.5%
in 2020. At the sectoral level, loans granted to individuals for
consumption had a share of 60.7% of the total loans granted in
MDL to individuals, while loans granted for the purchase of real
estate – 31.8%.
In 2021,
the volume of loans granted in foreign currency
recalculated in MDL was 2.6 times lower than that of loans
granted in the national currency.
The total volume of loans
granted in foreign currency, including foreign-currency-linked
ones, recalculated in MDL recorded MDL 12,416.3 million, higher
than in 2020 by 22.1% (Chart 3.12). Like in the previous year, the
majority
share
of
the
volume
of
foreign
currency
loans
recalculated in MDL and foreign-currency-linked loans belongs to
legal entities (98.2%).
Deposit market
At the end of 2021, the annual variation of the total balance of
deposits was 13.2%, lower than on 31 December 2020 by 2.9
percentage points (Chart 3.13), due to the reduction in the annual
growth rate of sight deposits (Chart 3.14). The acceleration of
economic growth in 2021 determined the evolution of the balance
of sight deposits, which, at the end of the year registered a growth
rate of 18.9% compared to 32.7% at the end of 2020. The annual
dynamics of term deposits constituted 6.6%, higher than on 31
December 2020 by 5.1 percentage points. Like in 2020, at the end
of 2021, the balance of sight deposits exceeded the balance of
term deposits. Thus, the balance of sight deposits was 56.4% on
31 December 2021.
From the perspective of the structure of the balance by currencies,
both components, in MDL and in foreign currency, recorded lower
annual dynamics compared to 2020 (Chart 3.13). As a result, on
31 December 2021, the dynamics of the balance of deposits in
MDL was 11.5%, lower than at the end of the previous year by
3.2 percentage points, and that of deposits in foreign currency
recalculated in MDL 15.6%, lower by 2.8 percentage points. Like
in previous years, at the end of 2021, the majority share was held
by the balance of deposits in the national currency, 58.3%. The
share of deposits attracted from individuals in the total balance of
deposits attracted in MDL was 64.3% at the end of the reporting
year.
Evolution of new term deposits attracted by the licensed
banks
In 2021, the new term deposits attracted by licensed banks had an
upward trend compared to the previous year. The annual dynamics
of the total volume of new term deposits attracted was 18.4%,
with a volume of MDL 29,396.6 million. In 2021, the volume of
deposits increased in both the national currency segment and in
foreign currency recalculated in MDL (Charts 3.15 and 3.16).

in MDL increased by 21.5%, due to the evolution of deposits
attracted from individuals and legal entities. Term deposits in
foreign currency recalculated in MDL attracted from individuals
had a share of 85.0%.
Interest rates on new deposits and loans granted by the
licensed banks
In January-June 2021, the Executive Board of the National Bank of
Moldova maintained the base rate applied to the main monetary
policy operations at the level established in November 2020, of
2.65% annually. Subsequently, starting at the end of July 2021,
the Executive Board of the National Bank of Moldova began the
series of increases in the base rate applied to the main short-term
monetary policy operations. As a result, there were ﬁve rounds of
increases, by 1.0 percentage points each in July and September,
a total of 1.85 percentage points in October and 1.0 percentage
points in December.
Thus, at the end of 2021, the base rate applied to the main
monetary policy operations was 6.50% annually. Interest rates on
new term deposits and on new loans granted in national currency
by licensed banks only increased by the end of the reporting year
(Chart 3.17).
Interest rates on new loans granted by the licensed banks
The nominal weighted average interest rate on new loans granted
in MDL in 2021 was 7.40% annually, lower than in 2020 by 0.75
percentage points (Chart 3.11).
The minimum value of the
weighted average interest rate on new loans granted in MDL was
recorded in August, and the maximum rate in December 2021
(Chart 3.17). For 2021, the weighted average interest rate on new
loans granted in MDL to legal entities was 8.24% annually, lower
than in 2020 by 0.25 percentage points, and the weighted
average interest rate on loans granted to individuals was 6.53%
annually or 1.1 percentage points less than the previous year.
For the whole of 2021, the weighted average rate on loans granted
in foreign currency was 4.04% annually, lower than in 2020 by
0.16 percentage points (Chart 3.12). Like in the previous year, the
rate reduction was inﬂuenced by interest rates on loans granted
to legal entities, which share prevailed.

In 2021, interest rates on new term deposits attracted by licensed
banks ranged from 3.11% annually to 4.13% annually in December
2021 (Chart 3.17). The weighted average rate of term deposits
in national currency in 2021 was 3.48% annually, lower than
in 2020 by 0.34 percentage points (Chart 3.15).
Its evolution
was largely determined by the weighted average interest rate on
deposits in MDL attracted from individuals, which share was 66.5%.
The weighted average interest rate on deposits attracted from
individuals in national currency was 3.95% annually, lower than in
2020 by 0.37 percentage points.
The weighted average interest rate on term deposits in foreign
currency, for the year 2021, was 0.47% annually (historically
minimum level), lower by 0.35 percentage points than in 2020
(Chart 3.16).
Banking margin on new operations
In 2021, in the domestic currency segment, the banking margin
on new operations in MDL decreased by 0.41 percentage points
against the previous year, registering a value of 3.92 percentage
points, due to greater reductions in interest rates on loans granted
than on term deposits.
In the foreign exchange segment, the
banking margin registered a slight increase and amounted to 3.57
percentage points (Chart 3.18).
Interest rates on loan and deposit balances
In addition to the impact of the decrease, during 2021, of interest
rates on new transactions and the situation created on the money
market, the weighted average interest rates on the balance in MDL
of both term deposits and loans granted decreased (Chart 3.19).
The weighted average interest rate on the balance of loans granted
in national currency as at the end of 2021 was 7.33% annually,
lower than at the end of 2020 by 0.33 percentage points. The
weighted average interest rate on the balance of foreign currency
loans and loans pegged to a foreign currency exchange rate was
4.22% annually, lower than at the end of 2020 by 0.65 percentage
points.
The weighted average interest rate on the balance of term deposits
in MDL, as at the end of 2021, was 3.74% annually, lower by 0.22
percentage points against the previous year. In turn, the weighted
average interest rate on the balance of foreign currency deposits
decreased compared to the end of 2020 by 0.29 percentage points
and constituted 0.43% annually.
At the end of 2021, bank margins on deposit and loan balances
decreased. The banking margin on the balance in national currency
was 3.59 percentage points compared to 3.70 percentage points in
2020. The banking margin on foreign currency balances was 3.79
percentage points compared to 4.15 percentage points in 2020.
Monetary indicators
After a money velocity deceleration in 2020, it accelerated in 2021.
The acceleration trend was speciﬁc throughout the year and was
determined by the increase in real economic activity, but also by


52
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
a higher growth rate of nominal GDP compared to the growth rate
of monetary aggregates.
The velocity of M1 monetary aggregate increased by 0.36
percentage points, registering a level of 4.0 points (Chart 3.20),
and the velocity ratio of M2 monetary aggregate accelerated by
0.28 percentage points, up to 2.9 points. At the same time, the
velocity of M3 monetary aggregate recorded 2.0 points, being
0.16 percentage points above the level recorded in the previous
year.

In the last years, money multipliers have continuously increased
and, throughout 2021, they have remained on an upward trend.
The trend was driven by an increase in the demand for loans
from the real sector of the national economy, acceleration of the
velocity of money, the increase in the demand for consumer loans
from individuals, as well as the increase in public sector spending.
In 2021, the M3 money multiplier had the highest increase – by
0.23 percentage points up to 2.56 points (Chart 3.21), M2 money
multiplier increased by 0.14 percentage points up to 1.77 points,
and the M1 money multiplier reached the level of 1.30 points,
being 0.11 percentage points above the level recorded in the
previous year.
After an accelerated growth during 2020 and the ﬁrst part of 2021,
the monetization coefﬁcient of the economy had a decelerating
trend in all components at the end of 2021. This development was
determined by the stimulative/tightening cycle of monetary policy
during 2020-2021. Thus, at the end of 2021, the M3 monetization
coefﬁcient reached 0.50 points (Chart 3.22), being 0.04 percentage
points below last year’s level. The M2 monetization coefﬁcient
recorded 0.34 points, being, likewise, 0.04 percentage points below
the level of 2020. The M0 monetization coefﬁcient decreased by
0.02 percentage points compared to 2020 and, at the end of the
year, recorded the level of 0.13 points.
The
economy
indebtedness
degree
during
2021
remained
unchanged compared to the previous year and constituted 24.4%.
The economy indebtedness remained at the same level because
the growth rate of loans granted by the banking sector coincided
with the growth rate of economic activity.
At the same time, the economy lending coefﬁcient (loan-deposit
ratio) increased during 2021 by 4.3 percentage points, up to
67.0% (Chart 3.23), because loans had higher growth rates than
deposits. It should be noted that the trends in indebtedness degree
and economy lending coefﬁcient were mainly determined by the
incentive measures of the monetary policy aiming at encouraging
investments and consumption over saving.
The degree of dollarization of the balance of loans granted in the
economy maintained its downward trend and, at the end of 2021,
constituted 31.0% of the total balance of loans, lower than at the
end of the previous year by 5.1 percentage points, due to the more
pronounced increase in lending in national currency during 2021.
The dollarization of deposits registered a minor increase of 0.9
percentage points and, at the end of the year, constituted 41.7%
(Chart 3.24).

3.5
Monetary market
Chart 3.25: Quantitative indicators of the primary

Primary market of state securities
As a state agent responsible for the organisation of state securities
(SS) placement in book-entry form, the National Bank organised
and carried out 125 SS placement auctions in 2021.
Given the substantial increase in the ﬁnancing needs of the
Ministry of Finance, the constraints generated by the uncertainty
of the release of external ﬁnancing tranches, in the electoral
context of 2021, the Ministry of Finance signiﬁcantly increased the
volumes of SS offered at auctions, intervening in the SS maturity
structure as well. Thus, in addition to those traditionally proposed
for sale, SS with a maturity of 7 years were launched on the
market for the ﬁrst time, starting from the second quarter of the
year.
However, in the speciﬁc conditions of 2021, investors’ appetite for
these types of investments was modest. The requested volume
exceeded the indicative one only in the ﬁrst two months of the
year, after which it fell deeply below it until the end of the year.
Even under these conditions, the Ministry of Finance managed to
place a historically record volume of MDL 25,262.8 million over
the entire period (Chart 3.25).
Interest rates on the SS market were quasi stable in the ﬁrst eight
months, oscillating around 5.00%, after which they followed a
rapid upward trend, reaching this year’s maximum of 8.99% in
December, being extremely higher than the base rate of 6.50%
(Chart 3.26). The rise was driven again mainly by changes in
the NBM base rate, to which more modest inﬂuences from other
segments added: investors’ interest in this type of placements
and the substantial increase in the Ministry of Finance’s need for
funding.
Non-bank investors were still present on the SS market, attracted
by SS interest rates, and they purchased the same volumes during
2021 as in 2020 (MDL 1,863.7 million) in absolute value, but
registering a decrease as a share in the total awarded volume
(from 10.2% to 7.4%) compared to 2020.
In the speciﬁc conditions of 2021, investors, expressing their risk
aversion, opted for SS with a circulation term of up to one year,
these having the largest share in the total SS issued. No broad
movements in the maturity structure were attested, but a more
signiﬁcant decrease in the share of government bonds. As a result,
the average maturity of issued securities decreased from 314 days
to 281 days (Charts 3.27 and 3.28).

In the ﬁrst eight months of 2021, the SS interest rates maintained
the same pattern speciﬁc to the second half of the previous year,
after which they rose sharply and became very close in value.
The interest rates recorded at the end of the year were 8.73%,
9.41%, and 9.45% annually for maturities of 91, 182 and 364 days,
respectively, being clearly higher than the values recorded at the
end of 2020 and reﬂecting expectations for a continued monetary
policy interest rate increase cycle in the immediately following
period (Chart 3.29).
Nevertheless, the pronounced increases from the last quarter of
2021 were less felt in the annual average related to the awarded
SS, which value increased by only half a percentage point for the
full year 2021. Thus, the amplitude of the increases was similar in
terms of securities’ maturity, recording adjustments of comparable
sizes for all maturities: 0.66, 0.61, and 0.43 percentage points,
respectively, for securities with a circulation term of 91, 182, and
364 days, respectively.
The nominal annual weighted average interest rates on SS traded
over the last two years in the primary market auctions, split by
maturity, are shown in table 3.1.
In the hierarchy of returns on the ﬁnancial market, interest on
loans in the economy continued to maintain their primacy. The
gains from investments in state securities were higher than those
from bank deposits. However, the interest rates on SS resumed
and then accelerated their growth in the last quarter of the year,
signiﬁcantly moving away from those on deposits and overtaking
the interest rates on loans in December (Chart 3.30).
The volume of state securities placed on the primary market
through auctions, in circulation as at 31.12.2021, recorded MDL
18,778.1 million at nominal value (Chart 3.31). At sale-purchase
price, it amounted to MDL 18,052.0 million, registering an increase
of MDL 3,981.5 million at the end of the reporting year compared to
the end of the previous year. Of the SS in circulation on 31.12.2021,
those with a residual maturity of up to three months constituted
28.5%, from 3 to 6 months – 35.5%, from 6 to 12 months – 24.8%,
over 1 year – 11.2%.
Secondary market of state securities
The quantitative parameters of the SS secondary market in 2021
continued the increase in value characteristic of the previous years
(Chart 3.32).
The total volume of secondary market transactions exceeded 1
billion, amounting to MDL 1,308.0 million, compared to MDL 988.0
million in the previous year, with the maximum monthly value
(MDL 179.9 million) being recorded in September.

The yields of state securities with a maturity of up to one year
traded on the secondary market followed the trend of the primary
market of the SS, which in turn were responsive to the evolution
of the NBM’s monetary policy rates. Thus, after a slight ﬂuctuation
around the value of 5.00% registered between January and
October, secondary market interest rates rose sharply in the last
two months of the year, reaching the maximum of 8.65% in
December.
The average annual interest rate was 5.55% (2020: 5.11%), while
the weighted average term decreased to 205 days (2020: 240
days).
As for the structure of SS traded on the secondary market, the
most traded are still the securities with a maturity of up to one
year (Charts 3.33 and 3.34). In this context, compared to the
previous year, one can observe the absence of T-bills with a term
of up to one month and 3-year GB in the curve, and at the same
time the investors’ preference for securities with a maturity of half
a year over those with a 3-month maturity.
Evolution of interbank market indicators in Moldovan lei
Reference
interest
rates
applied
on
interbank
money
market
The reference rate curve of CHIBOR/CHIBID (calculated based on
the indicative interest rates for placing/attracting funds in national
currency on the interbank money market) reacted promptly to the
changes in the NBM monetary policy rate in the second half of
2021.
The CHIBOR 2W yield curve remained relatively stable in the ﬁrst
eight months of the year, but mostly above the upper limit of
the rate corridor established by the NBM. Under the impact of
successive increases in the base rate, in early September it re-
entered an upward trajectory, but the amplitude of the increase
was modest. The rate growth accelerated temporarily in early
December, strongly absorbing the increase in the base rate during
that period, and continued to oscillate near the upper limit of the
interest rate corridor until the end of the year. The CHIBOR 2W rate
recorded at the end of the year was higher than the one recorded
on the last day of the previous year (+2.07 percentage points),
representing 8.19% (Chart 3.35).
Interbank loans/deposits market in MDL
In the context of tightening liquidity conditions on the money
market, banks’ recourse to the interbank market has increased, at
the
same
time,
the
role
of
interest
rates
on
interbank
loans/deposits in the monetary transmission mechanism remains
low.

For the full year 2021, a limited number of participants registered
transactions on the interbank loans/deposits market, all of which
were very short-term (overnight). The total volume of transactions
increased to MDL 662.0 million, from MDL 55.0 million in the
previous year, half of them coming from the last quarter of the
year, when there was a more signiﬁcant decrease in liquidity on
the interbank market.
The last months of the year saw the highest interest rates on the
interbank market (a maximum of 7.60% in December), with those
following the trend of the NBM monetary policy rates and, at the
same time, falling within the rate corridor limits.
The annual weighted average value of the yield on interbank
loans/deposits increased from 3.15% in the previous year to 4.87%
in 2021.

and
international

and

Compared to 2019, there was also a solid increase of 15.3%.

The turnover of transfer operations had a higher growth rate
against the previous year (+28.3%) than the turnover of cash
operations (+23.5%), thus contributing to the increase in the
share of turnover of the transfer foreign exchange market by up to
73.8% in total (Chart 3.36).
The structure by counterparty type of transfer operations remained
relatively unchanged compared to the previous year, the majority
share being still held by legal entities, with 93.0% of the operations’
turnover, while individuals accounted for 7.0%.
As to foreign exchange operations in cash, 63.3% were carried
out through the foreign exchange ofﬁces of licensed banks. After
a more pronounced decrease in the previous year, the turnover
of foreign exchange operations carried out by foreign exchange
ofﬁces recovered at a faster pace (+36.4%) than the turnover of
operations through foreign exchange bureaux of licensed banks
(+16.1%).
Euro continued to hold the majority share in the currency structure
of the total turnover of the domestic foreign exchange market –
53.3%, although it slightly decreased against the previous year
(-1.5 percentage points) (Charts 3.37 and 3.38). While in the cash
transaction segment of the foreign exchange market, the single
European currency continues to hold a dominant share with more
than 70.0% of turnover, the transfer transaction segment has a
more balanced structure, with the USD holding 49.3%, followed by
the EUR with 47.2% (Table A.6).
The single European currency maintained its dominant position as
number of transactions as well. About two thirds of the foreign
exchange operations with non-banking legal entities were against
the EUR (Chart 3.39).
EUR operations prevail in the number of transactions up to the
equivalent of USD 500 thousand, conversions with larger amounts
being still carried out mainly in USD (Charts 3.40 and Charts 3.41).
The turnover of foreign exchange operations against MDL carried
out by licensed banks amounted to USD 16,021.6 million,
increasing by 26.0% compared to the previous year. After a sharp
decrease in the previous year, the turnover of operations with
legal entities increased by 29.1%. Both purchases and sales to
economic operators increased in comparable proportions. At the
same time, the turnover of operations with individuals and foreign


58
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
exchange ofﬁces increased in smaller proportions, by 18.5%, with
a more pronounced increase in sales (+44.5%) than purchases
(+13.2%).
The interbank segment of the domestic foreign exchange market
also recorded positive developments, both between resident banks
(+40.6%) and with non-resident banks (+141.6%). In this sense,
the share of interbank transactions in the total turnover of licensed
banks increased to 5.4%, compared to 4.5% in the previous year.

In 2021, the domestic foreign exchange market saw a foreign
currency deﬁcit, with net purchases from individuals, foreign
exchange ofﬁces, and other sources (USD 2,629.5 million) being
below the level of net demand from economic operators (USD
2,949.5 million28). Under these conditions, the NBM intervened
through net sales of foreign currency to the licensed banks in
amount of USD 305.4 million during the year. In the ﬁrst semester,
a surplus of foreign currency was recorded and the NBM made net
purchases of foreign currency, respectively, while in the second
semester, as the demand for foreign currency for the import of
energy resources increased, the NBM intervened through net sales
(Table A.7).
The dynamics of foreign exchange assets and liabilities of
the licensed banks
During
the
reporting
year,
the
balance-sheet
foreign
exchange assets and liabilities of licensed banks increased
by 11.7% each. Thus, on 31 December 2021, their balances were
USD 2,391.9 million and USD 2,392.5 million,
respectively.
Excluding exchange rate differences, the increase was higher –
18.4% and 18.5%, respectively. Under these conditions, foreign
currency assets and liabilities slightly increased as share in total
bank assets and liabilities, up to 35.5% and 42.9%, compared to
2020.
Balance-sheet foreign currency assets and liabilities remain
mostly denominated in euros. In 2021, the share of this currency
continued to strengthen, holding just over 3/4 in the structure of
both indicators (Charts 3.42 and 3.44).
In the structure of assets by elements, the positive dynamics of
banks’ available funds emerged, which amounted to USD 866.9
million at the end of the reporting year, increasing by 24.1%
against the previous year, and by 30.5% excluding exchange
rate differences (Chart 3.43). Available funds in foreign currency
accounted for 36.2% in total balance-sheet foreign currency assets.
In particular, the funds held on "Nostro" accounts opened with
banks abroad increased in balance (Tables A.8 and A.9).
As to the currency structure of local banks’ available funds, as
of 31 December 2021, the majority share went to the European
single currency – 74.4%, followed by the US dollar – 21.7%, the
Russian ruble – 0.9%, and other currencies – 3.0%.
28The amount also includes some currency conversions by a licensed bank with a
non-resident bank on behalf of a local company with foreign capital

During 2021, the foreign currency lending activity of licensed
banks slightly increased, evolving differently depending on the
currency of the loan. Thus, the portfolio of loans granted in USD
(including those granted in MDL pegged to the exchange rate of
the MDL against the USD) increased by 27.7%, while the portfolio
of loans granted in EUR (including those granted in MDL pegged to
the exchange rate of the MDL against the EUR) increased in smaller
proportions, by 5.3% (excluding exchange rate differences), and
contracted by 2.2% at the effective exchange rate.
Under these conditions, compared to the end of 2020, the balance
of foreign currency loans (including those pegged to the foreign
currency exchange) increased by 9.6% (excluding exchange rate
differences) or by 2.7% (effective rate), up to USD 929.4 million (of
which USD 108.1 million pegged to the foreign currency exchange
rate). The balance of loans in foreign currency did not exceed
42.1% in the structure of foreign currency balance-sheet assets,
and, at the end of the reporting year, it stood at 38.9% – about 3.4
percentage points less than on the last day of 2020.
As for liabilities, their increase was generated by banks attracting
funds in euro (+15.1%, excluding exchange rate differences), for
US dollars the increase being insigniﬁcant (+1.0%).
In the structure of balance-sheet foreign currency liabilities of the
licensed banks, 90.1% went to the traditional ones: term and sight
deposits29 of non-bank customers (hereinafter – foreign currency
accounts). At the end of 2021, the balance of foreign exchange
accounts amounted to USD 2,155.6 million – up 11.9% and by
excluding exchange rate differences - 18.5% compared to the end
of 2020 (Chart 3.45, Tables A.10 and A.11).
In 2021, bank customers, both individuals and legal entities,
preferred to increase their deposits in sight accounts, both in
euro and in US dollars, over the term deposits. Respectively, the
balance of funds held on customers’ sight accounts increased by
19.6%, up to USD 1,475.6 million, and the balance of the term
ones decreased by 1.9% to USD 680.0 million.
However, foreign currency term deposits attracted from non-bank
customers of banks, mostly individuals, covered in signiﬁcant
proportions, 83.2%, loans granted in foreign currency by licensed
banks (excluding those pegged to the foreign currency exchange
rate).
As of 31 December 2021, banks’ foreign currency liabilities to
individuals decreased,
against 31 December 2020,
by 2.4
percentage points as a share of the total balance-sheet foreign
currency liabilities, although their balance increased by 7.1%
(+13.6%, excluding exchange rate differences) to USD 1,334.7
million. This dynamic was due to a more pronounced increase in
the balance of foreign currency accounts of non-banking legal
entities, by 20.7% (+27.5%, excluding exchange rate differences),
29Current accounts and sight deposits


60
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
up to USD 820.9 million, mainly, against the background of a
temporary phenomenon of conversion of MDL savings into foreign
currency, observed on the local foreign exchange market, as a
reaction to the emergence of inﬂationary trends. In this regard, at
the end of the reporting year, the balance of the foreign currency
accounts of individuals and non-bank legal entities amounted to
55.8% and 34.3%, respectively (+2.5 percentage points) in total
balance-sheet foreign currency liabilities of licensed banks.

As to the dynamics of the currencies of countries with signiﬁcant
shares in the foreign trade of the Republic of Moldova and,
respectively, included in the REER basket, those of the EU
member states, including the pound sterling and the Chinese
renminbi strengthened against the US dollar, and the remaining
currencies lost ground to it, with depreciations greater than that
of the Moldovan leu being registered by the Turkish lira – 23.9%,
the Belarusian ruble – 4.5%, and the Russian ruble – 2.5% (Chart
3.47). In this aspect, in 2021, the Moldovan leu depreciated, on
average, by 2.3% in nominal terms and by 2.9% in real terms
against the currencies of the countries – main trading partners,
with depreciation being determined by all currencies included in
the REER basket, except for the Turkish lira (Charts 3.48 and 3.49).
According to year-end values, the MDL appreciated by 10.3% in
real terms.
The year 2021 was characterized by the relaunch of domestic
demand, including for imported products, as well as by the series
of price increases on external markets of energy resources, food

products, and raw materials, which affected the level of prices
in Moldova and the perception of market participants concerning
the purchasing power of the MDL. The trade balance deteriorated
by 37.6%, while the import of goods and services increased by
33.6%, and the export – by 30.3%.

exchange came also from some companies with foreign capital
that distributed part of the ﬁnancial result to foreign investors,
with dividend payments increasing by 15.5% and reaching a new
all-time high. Therefore, the net demand for foreign exchange
from economic operators (hereinafter – demand) amounting to
USD 2,949.5 million increased by 28.3% compared to 2020, that
from importers of energy resources increasing signiﬁcantly, by
59.3%. At the same time, the net supply of foreign currency from
individuals (hereafter – supply) grew more slowly in 2021, by 4.1%,
with the 8.0% growth in net personal remittances explaining this
increase.
Under these circumstances, in 2021, the foreign exchange market
operated in conditions of net foreign exchange demand.
The
degree of demand coverage by supply dropped to 80.8% against
99.6% in the previous year (Chart 3.50), this dynamic creating
conditions for the NBM’s interventions of net sale of currency in a
volume of USD 305.4 million.
A minimum value of the degree of demand coverage by supply
of 23.7%, was reached in November 2021, when the amendment
of the contractual conditions for the import of natural gas from
the Russian Federation resulted in a major increase in prices,
causing inﬂationary expectations and determining the temporary
preference of individuals and legal entities for foreign currency
savings. To prevent the destabilization of the foreign exchange
market and a strong volatility of the national currency rate, the
NBM covered the needs of the foreign exchange market by selling
USD 237.1 million, which constituted about 77.6% of the total net
selling interventions made by the NBM for the full year 2021.
In 2021, there were also temporary periods of currency surplus
and, respectively, a reversal of the exchange rate trend towards
an appreciation determined by the seasonal supply of currency
during the summer period and holidays, but also by some foreign
currency inﬂows through direct foreign investments, especially
in the retail sector and through external ﬁnancing, especially in
favour of the public administration.
The net supply of foreign currency from individuals continued to
be denominated, predominantly, in euros (74.6%30), the share of
other currencies, mostly the pound sterling, being on an obvious
increase (Chart 3.51).
Ofﬁcial reserve assets
According to the situation at the end of the year, ofﬁcial reserve
assets amounted to USD 3,901.9 million, increasing by 3.1%
compared to the end of 2020, due to important ﬂows of ﬁnancial
assistance from the main development partners of the Republic
of Moldova. In this regard, the following external credit and grant
inﬂows were noted:

the economic consequences caused by the pandemic, with

At the same time, in December, following
the conclusion of a new program of economic and ﬁnancial
policies with the Republic of Moldova, the IMF disbursed the
ﬁrst tranche of budget support, in the form of a loan in the
amount equivalent to USD 79.8 million;

proportions the actual import of goods and services – about 5.9
months of import.
Managing foreign exchange reserves
In accordance with Art. 5, 16, 53 and 71 of Law No. 548/1995 on
the National Bank of Moldova, the NBM maintains and manages the
foreign exchange reserves of the state, performs foreign exchange
operations using foreign exchange reserves and maintains them at
an appropriate level for the conduct of the monetary and foreign
exchange policy of the state. The role of foreign exchange reserves
is to ensure the credibility and ﬁnancial stability of the country, to
honour the payments of the state’s external liabilities and to deal
with unpredictable exogenous shocks.
In the process of managing foreign exchange reserves, the NBM
ensures a high degree of security and a necessary level of liquidity
of investments. The NBM’s investment policy is prudent, aiming
at optimizing proﬁtability only as a secondary objective, while
liquidity and security of reserves is essential.
A fundamental
element of the process of managing foreign exchange reserves
is investment risk management, which is achieved by imposing
constraints and limits on investment.
Table A.13 presents the main risks related to the management
of foreign exchange reserves and techniques applied to reduce
exposure.


Chapter 3. Monetary Policy
63
Starting with July 2013, the Strategic Asset Allocation (SAA) has
been implemented in collaboration with the World Bank. Strategic
Asset Allocation involves a long-term asset management vision
focused on achieving optimal proﬁtability and risk. In the context
of the SAA, international reserves are divided into three tranches
(current,
liquidity,
and
investment)
depending
on
speciﬁc
objectives and regulations, this approach ensuring the more
efﬁcient achievement of the set objective.
The rapid spread of the COVID-19 virus during 2020 led monetary
authorities worldwide to use a wide range of measures and take
monetary policy decisions to mitigate the effects of the pandemic
on economies. The year 2021 was notable for increasing prospects
of vaccination against COVID-19, but uncertainty remained, being
determined by the inability to forecast the subsequent trajectory of
propagation, especially in the context of occurrence of new strains
of the virus, as well as the economic impact and time required for
recovery.
The process of managing foreign exchange reserves is conditioned
by the evolution of the world economy, by the decisions of the
major central banks of the world, especially by the monetary
authorities of the USA, the Eurozone, and Great Britain, considering
the importance of the currencies of these countries in the structure
of ofﬁcial reserve assets.
In this aspect, the monetary authorities of these countries opted,
in most of the monetary policy meetings held in 2021, for
maintaining the monetary policy rates. Thus, the reference rate of
the US Federal Reserve stood in the range of 0-0.25%, that of the
European Central Bank – at the level of 0%, and that of the Bank
of England – at 0.10%, being increased to 0.25% only on 15
December 2021 in the context of inﬂationary pressures.

The evolution trend of these economies is also shown in the
dynamics of yields on government bonds (Chart 3.52).
The
evolution of the monetary policy rates established by the
monetary authorities is presented in the Chart 3.53.
These
developments
had
a
signiﬁcant
impact
on
the
ﬁnancial
performance of the National Bank in terms of diminishing the
proﬁtability of foreign reserves.
Ofﬁcial reserve assets, as of 31 December 2021 compared to 31
December 2020, increased by 3.1% or approximately USD 118
million (from USD 3,784 million to USD 3,902 million).
In the
management of foreign exchange reserves, the investment policy
of the National Bank is a prudent one, the assets being allocated in
safe instruments, also used by other central banks: placements on
correspondent accounts (usually with other central banks), term
placements and securities, which are classiﬁed into supranational
(issued by supranational institutions), governmental (issued by the
US government, governments of European Union member states
and other government issuers), and non-governmental (issued by
agencies) securities.
Although in the context of the pandemic and the low level of
interest rates, an increase in the investors’ risk appetite was noted,
the National Bank showed vigilance towards risk exposure. In
this regard, the adjustment of the related regulatory framework
imposed additional limits and constraints to reduce risk exposure
under conditions of uncertainty.


64
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Some of the foreign exchange reserves are managed externally
by the World Bank. In accordance with the Investment Advisory
and Management Agreement between the International Bank for
Reconstruction and Development (IBRD) and the National Bank
of Moldova of 8 December 2010 (extended by DEB No. 168 of
30 December 2015), IBRD is the NBM’s consultant and agent for
the administration of part of foreign assets, limited to 20% of
foreign exchange reserves. As of 31 December 2020, the share of
externally managed assets accounted for 5.6% of foreign exchange
reserves.
In 2021, considering the unfavourable situation on ﬁnancial
markets, the portfolio of securities valued at amortized cost
(previously, the portfolio of securities held until maturity) was not
supplemented with new securities. The value of this portfolio, on
31 December 2021, was approximately USD 167 million or 4.3%
of the foreign exchange reserves, the maximum level of the
portfolio being limited to 25.0% of the reserves.
As at the end of the reporting year, 47.2% of the reserves are
invested in securities, 18.6% are term investments, and 34.2% are
held in cash and available funds on correspondent accounts (Chart
3.54).

The
return
on
investment
depends
on
market
conditions,
exchange rate ﬂuctuations and investment instrument yields,
which are closely correlated with monetary policy decisions and
other stimulus packages that have tended to tip the balance in
one direction or another, subject to reaction speed, policy
adaptation, transmission, intended effect, etc. By the end of 2021,
higher volatility in the main currency pairs was shaping up amid
heightened speculation on monetary policy measures and the
uncertain macroeconomic outlook.
Additionally,
it became
increasingly obvious that the increase in global inﬂation would not
be transitory as previously anticipated. The increase in the prices
of energy resources due to the recovery of demand and its
non-correlation with supply, in terms of the major impact on
consumption, was an impediment in the global recovery. Thus,
signiﬁcant inﬂationary pressures led monetary authorities to
adjust their monetary policy decisions in a way that would cope
with those pressures for a longer period.
The evolution of the global economic environment inﬂuenced
investment decisions and currency composition of reserves. In this
context, the majority share (according to year-end values) in the
structure of foreign exchange reserves belonged to the US dollar –
63.44% (Chart 3.55).
At the same time, the currency composition of the reserves falls
within the permissible deviation limits of +/-10 percentage points
from the normative currency structure.
Based on the evolution of the world economy in 2021, particularly,
of the USA, EU, and United Kingdom, and the situation on the
ﬁnancial markets and dynamics of exchange rates against the US
dollar (reserve reporting currency), the management of foreign
reserves generated a proﬁt of USD 4.89 million, at an average
proﬁtability rate of 0.13%.


65
Chapter 4
Banking supervision
4.1
Evolution of the banking sector
Core indicators of the banking system
As of 31.12.2021, in the Republic of Moldova there were 11 banks
licensed by the National Bank of Moldova, including 5 branches of
foreign banks and ﬁnancial groups.
The total number of subdivisions of licensed banks in the Republic
of Moldova amounted to 636, of which 289 branches and 347
agencies.
As of 31.12.2021, 8,282 persons were working in the banking
sector. On average, each employee managed assets worth MDL
14.3 million, an increase of MDL 1.4 million (10.6% ) compared to
the end of 2020.
The ﬁnancial situation of the banking sector as at 31.12.2021
was satisfactory. Banks are adequately capitalized, own funds
amounted to MDL 15,158.4 million, increasing by MDL 1,532.5
million (11.2%) compared to the previous year, and the total own
funds ratio remains at a high level, recording a value of 25.9%,
decreasing by 1.4 percentage points compared to 2020 due to
the increase in risk exposures. According to reports submitted by
banks, all banks met the "Total own funds ratio" indicator (limit
for each bank ≥10.0%) and additional requirements imposed by
supervisory measures.
The dynamics of the main ﬁnancial indicators are shown in chart
4.1.
Chart 4.1: Dynamics of main ﬁnancial indicators of the
banking sector of the Republic of Moldova for the period
31.12.2020* - 31.12.2021

*Data as of 31.12.2020 throughout the text are adjusted
according to the results of the external audit.
Proﬁt relating to the year ended 31.12.2021 amounted to MDL
2,301.9 million in the sector, increasing from the previous year
by MDL 800.7 million (53.3%), due to the increase in interest
income by MDL 739.2 million (16.9%), income from fees and
commissions – by MDL 545.5 million (27.6%), as well as the
decrease in impairment of ﬁnancial assets not measured at fair
value through proﬁt or loss in the amount of MDL 386.6 million
(75.6%). At the same time, interest expenses decreased by MDL
71.0 million (5.4%) and non-interest expenses increased by MDL
449.6 million (9.0%).
For 2021, the return on assets31 and equity32 of banks recorded
values of 2.0% and 12.3%, respectively, increasing by 0.5

percentage points and 3.6 percentage points,
respectively,
compared to the previous year.
Total assets amounted to MDL 118,534.2 million, increasing by
MDL 14,760.6 million (14.2%) during 2021.
As at 31.12.2021,
the balance of the gross loan portfolio
(prudential) accounted for 47.5% of total assets or MDL 56,359.2
million, increasing by MDL 10,716.0 million (23.5%) during the
period under review.
At the same time, non-performing loans
increased by MDL 90.8 million (2.7%) to MDL 3,460.5 million,
while the share of non-performing loans in total loans decreased
by 1.2 percentage points to 6.1%.
The balance of deposits increased during 2021 by MDL 10,443.2
million (13.1%) to MDL 90,081.9 million.
Own funds
As of 31.12.2021, own funds in the sector, according to the data
provided by banks, amounted to MDL 15,158.4 million, recording
an increase of MDL 1,532.5 million (11.2%) during the year.
The increase in own funds was due to the reﬂection by banks of
eligible proﬁts after holding general meetings of shareholders and
after obtaining the NBM’s permission to include in own funds the
realized proﬁts in the total amount of MDL 1,405.2 million. At the
same time, other intangible assets reduced by MDL 136.5 million,
as well as the positive difference between allowances for losses on
assets and conditional commitments (prudential allowances) and
allowances for impairment losses on assets and provisions (IFRS
allowances) by MDL 97.5 million.
In 2021, given that the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic
persisted
creating
uncertainties
on
the
external
economic
environment of banks, as well for licensed banks to maintain an
adequate level of own funds and liquidity to ensure their viability
and stability, in the context of the after-effects of the COVID-19
pandemic, the NBM recommended banks to apply a prudent
approach to the decision to distribute dividends, to thoroughly
analyse macroeconomic circumstances, stress test results and
capital adequacy, taking into account the materialised impact and
after-effects of the pandemic on asset quality. If, following the
analysis carried out, it was considered appropriate to distribute
capital in the form of dividends, banks were recommended to limit
them to no more than 25% of the accumulated proﬁt for the years
2019 and 2020.
Respectively,
considering the mentioned
recommendations, during the reporting year, 5 banks (OTP Bank
S.A., BC "MOLDOVA-AGROINDBANK" S.A., BC "Moldindconbank"
S.A., "FinComBank" S.A., BC "ProCredit Bank" S.A.) distributed
capital (dividends) in the total amount of MDL 636.5 million.
The structure of total own funds is made up entirely of common
equity Tier 1 capital in 9 banks (total by sector – MDL 14,740.1
million), while 2 banks (BC "MOLDOVA-AGROINDBANK" S.A. and

As of 31.12.2021, the total own funds ratio for the banking sector
was 25.9% increasing by 1.4 percentage points compared to the
end of the previous year. The given indicator is observed by all
banks (limit for each bank ≥10%). At the same time, the banks
also comply with the requirement for the "Total own funds ratio"
indicator, taking into account capital buffers. Thus, in addition
to the minimum own funds requirement of 10%, the banks are
required to maintain common equity Tier 1 capital necessary
to meet the requirements for the capital conservation buffer
(2.5%), the countercyclical capital buffer (0.0%), the systemic risk
buffer (1%/3%) and, where applicable, the systemically important
company buffer (0.5%/1.0%/1.5%) (O-SII buffer).
At the same time, during 2021, the Executive Board of the NBM
approved the results of the supervision and assessment process
of banks’ activities,
with the imposition of speciﬁc capital
requirements to each bank according to its individual risk proﬁle,
which were respected by all the banks.
The dynamics of common equity Tier 1 capital and total own funds
over the period under review are shown in chart 4.2.
The change in the capital structure of the banking sector in terms
of its concentration in groups of banks is shown in chart 4.3.
The share of large banks’ total own funds in the banking sector’s
total own funds was 75.5%, increasing by 2.0 percentage points
compared to the end of the previous year.
At the same time, the share of total own funds of medium-sized
and small banks amounted to 20.8% and 3.7%, decreasing by 1.8
percentage points and 0.2 percentage points respectively.
The signiﬁcant share of foreign investment in the banks’ capital
was maintained during the analysed period, which as of 31.12.2021
amounted to 86.6% (Chart 4.4), being at the same level as at the
end of the previous year.
Among the foreign investors participating in the capital formation
of banks in the Republic of Moldova are banks from Italy, Hungary,
Romania, as well as the European Bank for Reconstruction and
Development,
and corporate investors from Germany,
the
Netherlands, Bulgaria, Austria, the United Kingdom, Greece, Iraq,
and Russia.
Out of the total number of banks, 4 banks have capital consisting
entirely of foreign investment (including 3 subsidiaries of foreign
banks and ﬁnancial groups: B.C. "EXIMBANK" S.A., BCR Chisinau
S.A. and B.C. "ProCredit Bank" SA), 6 banks – with capital composed
of foreign and domestic investments, and one bank – with capital
composed entirely of domestic investments.


68
Annual Report (NBM, 2021)
Assets and their quality
Total assets by sector amounted to MDL 118,534.2 million,
increasing by MDL 14,760.6 million (14.2%) compared to the end
of the previous year. The main source of ﬁnancing of assets is
debt, which increased by MDL 13,208.2 million (15.4%) to MDL
99,104.8 million, mainly due to the increase in deposits attracted
by MDL 10,446.7 million (13.1%) to MDL 90,145.8 million. Another
source of asset ﬁnancing is capital, which increased by MDL
1,552.3 million (8.7%) to MDL 19,429.4 million, due to the proﬁt
achieved during the reference period.
During 2021, the share of assets in GDP decreased by 3.0
percentage points from 52.0% to 49.0% (Chart 4.8).
The concentration of banking sector assets is shown in chart 4.5.

Banking sector assets are concentrated in the group of large
banks (determined as such by the NBM). At the end of 2021, 4
banks (BC "MOLDOVA-AGROINDBANK" S.A., BC "Moldindconbank"
S.A., OTP Bank S.A., B.C. "VICTORIABANK" S.A.). The share of
assets of banks in this group amounted to 80.1% as of 31.12.2021,
increasing by 1.8 percentage points compared to the end of 2020.
The share of assets of banks in the medium-sized banks group
(BC "ProCredit Bank" S.A., BC "EXIMBANK" S.A., "FinComBank"
S.A., BC "ENERGBANK" S.A., BCR Chisinau S.A.) decreased by
1.7 percentage points to 16.9%, while the share of assets of
banks in the group of small banks (BC "COMERT, BANK" S.A., BC
"EuroCreditBank" S.A.) decreased by 0.1 percentage points to
3.0%. The composition of the groups at the end of 2021 remained
unchanged compared to the end of 2020.
It should be noted that the limits of the dominant position in
the banking market by asset size were respected. The largest
share of a bank’s total assets in relation to the total assets in the
banking sector belonged to BC "MOLDOVA-AGROINDBANK" S.A.,
being 31.4% (limit ≤35%), followed by BC "Moldindconbank" S.A. –
20.6% (Chart 4.6).
Following the analysis of the concentration of the banking sector
of the Republic of Moldova, the Herﬁndahl-Hirschmann index was
calculated (the value of which does not exceed 10,000), based on
the share of the bank’s assets in total assets in the banking sector.
This index is calculated as the sum of the squares of the market
shares of all banks in the sector. In the international practice, the
following division is used:
• HHI below 100 indicates a perfectly competitive market;
• HHI between 100 and 1,500 indicates a non-concentrated
market;
• HHI
between
1,500
and
2,500
indicates
a
moderate
concentration;
• HHI above 2,500 indicates a high concentration.


Chapter 4. Banking supervision
69
Thus, after the calculations made, obtaining HHI=1,870.6 we
conclude that the banking sector of the Republic of Moldova
presents a moderate concentration.

(MDL 54,646.8 million), increasing by 2.8 percentage points
compared to the corresponding period of the previous year. The
share of funds with the NBM amounted to 19.2% (MDL 22,725.5
million), decreasing by 2.5 percentage points. The share of banks’

The structure of banking sector assets by main components is
shown in Chart 4.7.
The gross loan portfolio (according to prudential reports) as at
31.12.2021 amounted to MDL 56,359.2 million, increasing during
the year by MDL 10,716.0 million (23.5%). The increase in the loan
portfolio was recorded monthly, except January.
At the same time, the gross loan portfolio’s share in GDP increased
by 0.4 percentage points, accounting for 23.3% as at 31.12.2021
(Chart 4.8).
In the context of the distribution of risks by branches, the largest
share in the total loan portfolio was held by loans granted to trade
– 21.5% (MDL 12,136.6 million), followed by the share of loans for
the purchase/construction of real estate – 20.3% (MDL 11,427.5
million) and the share of consumer loans – 18.6% (MDL 10,483.8
million).
The detailed structure of the loan portfolio of the banking sector
in the Republic of Moldova, according to the branch in which the
borrower operates, is reﬂected in the chart 4.9.
The largest increase in 2021 was recorded in loans for the
purchase/construction of real estate, which increased by MDL
3,547.1 million (45.0%) to MDL 11,427.5 million and in consumer
loans – by MDL 3,003.7 million (40.2%) to MDL 10,483.8 million.
It should be noted that signiﬁcant increases in the shares of these
branches in the sector may in the future generate certain risks
related to their concentration, including their recovery, especially
for consumer loans, which are largely granted without guarantees.
During 2021, non-performing loans (substandard, doubtful and
impaired) in absolute value increased by MDL 90.8 million (2.7%),
amounting to MDL 3,460.5 million.
At the same time, the share of non-performing loans in total loans
decreased by 1.2 percentage points compared to the end of 2020,
constituting 6.1% as at 31.12.2021. The improvement of the loan
portfolio quality indicator was due to the growth rate of the loan
portfolio being higher than that of non-performing loans.

by 0.2 percentage points, constituting 8.1% on 31.12.2021.

is reﬂected in charts 4.10 and 4.11:

allowances
on

in

non-performing loans) was high at 62.5%, ranging across banks
from 38.1% to 94.4% and down 3.0 percentage points from the
previous year.
The share of allowances calculated for loan losses in total loans as
at 31.12.2021 was 6.4%, decreasing by 1.1 percentage points
compared to the end of 2020.
It should be noted that the
allowances calculated on all assets and conditional commitments
as of 31.12.2021 amounted to MDL 4,788.5 million, and the
allowances for impairment losses formed amounted to MDL
3,217.0 million, the difference recorded – MDL 1,571.5 million.
The share of allowances for impairment losses on loans (IFRS
allowances) in the calculated allowances on loans (prudential
allowances) in the banking sector amounted to 73.0%, ranging
from 15.3% to 120.5% depending on the bank and increasing by
1.3 percentage points compared to the end of the previous year.
Banks’ exposures to related parties account for an insigniﬁcant
share of total loans – 1.3% (banking sector average). The indicator
"Ratio between exposures to related parties and eligible capital"
(limit for each bank ≤20% of eligible capital) as of 31.12.2021
was met by all banks (banking sector average – 4.8%).
As of 31.12.2021, the indicator "Ratio of the sum of the ten largest
net debts to the total portfolio of loans and contingent liabilities
(maximum limit for each bank – 30% of total net loans) was met
by all banks (average for the banking sector – 18.7%). At the same
time, only one bank exceeded the above-mentioned indicator,
which, according to Chapter IV item 26 of the Regulation on large
exposures No. 109 of 5 April 2019, is not considered a violation if
the bank maintains an additional own funds requirement on the
respective surplus.
The indicator of the total amount of the bank’s currency-linked
net exposures in MDL to individuals, including those engaged
33Net non-performing loans = the amount subject to classiﬁcation as non-

Chapter 4. Banking supervision
71
in entrepreneurial or other types of activity (limit for each bank
≤30% of eligible capital) was met by all banks. The sector average
of the nominated indicator was 2.6%.
The ratio of the total amount of net currency-linked non-mortgage
exposures to individuals to total regulatory capital (limit for each
bank ≤10% of eligible capital) was also met by all banks. The
sector average of the nominated indicator was 0.4%.
The ratio of total investments in long-term tangible ﬁxed assets
to total own funds in the sector was 18.8% (limit per bank ≤50%)
and was met by all banks.
The ratio of total investments in long-term tangible ﬁxed assets
and equity holdings in legal entities to total own funds in the
banking sector was 19.9% (limit for each bank ≤100%) and was
also respected by all banks.
Incomes and proﬁtability evolution
As of 31.12.2021, the proﬁt for the year for the banking sector
amounted to MDL 2,301.9 million. Compared to the end of the
previous year, the proﬁt increased by MDL 800.7 million (53.3%).
The increase in proﬁt was due to the increase in interest income by
MDL 739.2 million (16.9%), income from fees and commissions – by
MDL 545.5 million (27.6%), as well as the decrease in impairment
of ﬁnancial assets not measured at fair value through proﬁt or loss
in the amount of MDL 386.6 million (75.6%).
Interest income increased by MDL 739.2 million (16.9%) to MDL
5,107.0 million due to the considerable increase in the loan
portfolio during the period under review.
Non-interest income
increased by MDL 440.6 million (12.8%) to MDL 3,875.3 million,
mainly due to the increase in fee and commission income by MDL
545.5 million (27.6%) to MDL 2,519.2 million. At the same time,
losses from exchange rate differences were recorded in the
amount of MDL 96.2 million (7.6%) to MDL 1,171.7 million.

For 2021, the return on assets34 and return on equity35 of banks
recorded values of 2.0% and 12.3% accordingly, increasing by
0.5 percentage points and 3.6 percentage points respectively
compared to the end of the previous year.
Net interest margin36 amounted to 4.1% as at 31.12.2021, up 0.3
percentage points from the end of the previous year.
The dynamics of the net interest margin and return on assets and
capital of the banking sector of the Republic of Moldova for the
period 31.12.2020-31.12.2021 are shown in chart 4.15.
The average monthly value of interest-earning assets increased
during 2021 by MDL 12,038.6 million or 13.5% to MDL 101,179.7
million as at 31.12.2021. The signiﬁcant share of interest-earning
assets in total banking sector assets, which amounted to 85.3%,
shows the banks’ ability to generate income in the future.

liquidity
and

with
